the RND benchmark on \(100\) fresh seeds (\texttt{a\_seed} \(100\)--\(199\)),
plus the two visit-count oracle rows (\texttt{gt\_\allowbreak position\_\allowbreak velocity},
\(\beta{=}1\), fresh seeds \(200\)--\(499\)): the \(1/\sqrt{n}\)-decay count oracle
and the \(1/n\)-decay count oracle, the latter two measured on
the same training-episode metric so they are a proper ceiling for the learned
bonuses.  Sorted by \(\bar{R}\) descending; best \textbf{bold}, second
\underline{underlined} in \(\bar{R}\) and \(\mathrm{succ.}\).  ``\(\Delta\) vs
bench.'' is the gap to the RND-benchmark row and ``conf.'' the one-sided
confidence that the row exceeds the benchmark; the benchmark row is the
reference (``---'').  Neither SGD-\(1/t\) candidate significantly beats the
benchmark (the run-3.2.1 margin was selection bias), while both oracles are far
above it and the \(1/\sqrt{n}\) oracle beats the \(1/n\) oracle by about \(9\)
reward points (all \(300\) seeds finished for both oracles, zero failures).}
\label{tab:trainrun3-2-2-validation}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Where does the oracle's advantage come from? Isolating the even start.}
The count oracle's bonus \(\min(1, 1/\sqrt{n})\) differs from a learned RND bonus
in two separable ways:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*),leftmargin=*]
\item the \emph{numerator}: every unvisited cell starts at exactly \(1\) --- a
perfectly even initial bonus --- whereas a random network may already prefer
some states before any training;
\item the \emph{denominator}: the \(\sqrt{n}\) decay law.
\end{enumerate}
The two oracle rows of Table~\ref{tab:trainrun3-2-2-validation} isolate (2):
keeping the even start and changing only the decay from \(1/\sqrt{n}\) to
\(1/n\) costs about \(9\) reward points.  To isolate (1), the following plots
show the \emph{initial} bonus of the benchmark RND network --- the exact step-0
network of the \runref{run-3.2.2 benchmark}'s best seed (\texttt{a\_seed} \(111\), final
reward \(109.9\)), rebuilt by mirroring \texttt{train.py}'s construction (same
seeding order, so the weights are identical to that run's; the
observation-normalization warmup is re-seeded deterministically, and the
normalized field is insensitive to that draw: Pearson \(r=0.998\) across warmup
seeds).  The plotted value is the mse readout \(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{mse}}\) at
observation \((x, y, v_x, v_y)\); the env clips velocity to \(\pm 5\), so the
field is shown at the three levels \(v_x{=}v_y \in \{0, 2.5, 5\}\), normalized
by one shared maximum (the largest value across all three levels is \(1\)).
In every panel pair the left image is a surface over a dense \(100\times100\)
evaluation (no interpolation --- the network is evaluated densely) and the
right image an annotated heatmap of the \(10\times10\) sub-grid midpoints.
Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun3-2-2-initfield} shows the zero-velocity field: the
shared-normalization pair on top and, below it, the same field self-normalized
on a log scale, which exposes the relief that the shared linear scale
compresses.  Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun3-2-2-initfield-vel} shows the two higher
velocity levels, and Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun3-2-2-velsweep} sweeps each
velocity component at three maze positions.  Findings:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{The initial RND bonus is far from even.}  At zero velocity the
\(10\times10\) values span \(0.00\)--\(0.44\) (raw \(0.4\)--\(49\), a
\(>100\times\) spread): the field is a bowl centered near the maze middle ---
near-zero bonus at the center, growing toward the corners.  The mechanism is
the zero-bias orthogonal initialization: both predictor and target map the
normalized origin to zero, so the residual (hence the bonus) vanishes at the
observation-normalization center and grows with distance from it.  The oracle's
numerator starts even at \(1\); RND's starts with a strong, arbitrary spatial
preference --- so ingredient (1) genuinely separates the two, independent of the
decay law.
\item \textbf{Velocity dominates the initial bonus.}  Raising \(v_x{=}v_y\)
from \(0\) to \(5\) lifts the whole field from raw maximum \(49\) to \(111\)
(the shared normalized maximum \(1.00\) sits in the \(v{=}5\) panel); the
spatial bowl persists at every level but is small against the velocity effect.
The initial network therefore rewards \emph{fast} states more than \emph{new}
places.
\item \textbf{The two velocity components act with similar strength.}  The
per-position sweeps (Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun3-2-2-velsweep}) are roughly
quadratic with their minimum near \(v\approx 0\) and similar magnitude for
\(v_x\) and \(v_y\) (e.g.\ at the start cell the \(v_x\) sweep peaks at
\(51.5\) vs.\ \(46.4\) for \(v_y\)), with modest random asymmetries; at the
middle point the bonus at zero velocity is \(\approx 0\).
\end{enumerate}
\textbf{Both oracle ingredients differ from learned RND: the decay comparison
(\(1/\sqrt{n}\) vs.\ \(1/n\)) shows the \(\sqrt{n}\) denominator matters, and these
initial-field plots show the even numerator is also absent --- at step 0 RND
directs exploration by an arbitrary random preference for fast, corner states
rather than uniformly.}

\paragraph{How each implementation initializes the RND networks.}
The initial-bonus field above is a product of implementation choices, so
Table~\ref{tab:trainrun3-2-2-rnd-init} compares them across three
implementations: this project's, the original RND release
\citep{burda2019exploration} (the TensorFlow code at
\texttt{openai/\allowbreak random-network-distillation}, Atari path), and
CleanRL's \citep{huang2022cleanrl} \texttt{ppo\_\allowbreak rnd\_\allowbreak
envpool.py} (the file this project's \texttt{layer\_init} was taken from).
The striking agreement is the initialization law itself: \emph{all three draw
every weight of both networks from the same orthogonal initializer with gain
\(\sqrt{2}\) and set every bias to zero} --- so the zero-bias mechanism behind
the bowl of Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun3-2-2-initfield} is shared by all three.
The differences are architectural (the two reference implementations use a
\emph{deeper} predictor than target; this project uses identical shapes), in
the observation-statistics warmup (random-agent rollouts vs.\ this project's
observation-space samples), in the readout (mean vs.\ half-sum of squared
errors), and in whether the intrinsic reward is post-normalized at all.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.25}
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.6cm}
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.4cm}
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.6cm}
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Aspect} & \textbf{This work} & \textbf{Original RND (Burda et al.)} & \textbf{CleanRL} \\
\midrule

Target network & \(4 \to 256\) ReLU \(\to 128\) (linear out) & 3 conv (32/64/64, LeakyReLU) \(\to\) fc \(512\) (linear) & 3 conv (32/64/64, LeakyReLU) \(\to\) fc \(512\) (linear) \\
Predictor network & identical to the target & same convs \(+\) \emph{two extra} fc \(512\) ReLU layers before the \(512\) output & same convs \(+\) \emph{two extra} fc \(512\) ReLU layers before the \(512\) output \\
Weight init & orthogonal, gain \(\sqrt{2}\), every layer & orthogonal, gain \(\sqrt{2}\), every layer & orthogonal, gain \(\sqrt{2}\), every layer \\
Bias init & \(0\) & \(0\) & \(0\) \\
Output dim \(m\) & \(128\) & \(512\) & \(512\) \\
Obs.\ normalization & \((x-\mu)/\sigma\), clip \(\pm5\) & \((x-\mu)/\sigma\), clip \(\pm5\) & \((x-\mu)/\sigma\), clip \(\pm5\) \\
Obs.-stats warmup & \(200\) samples of the observation space (off-distribution) & random agent, \(6400\) env steps & random agent, \(50\) rollout iterations \\

networks.  Facts verified against this repository
(\texttt{methods/rnd.py}, \texttt{methods/\_\_init\_\_.py}), the original
TensorFlow release (\texttt{cnn\_\allowbreak policy\_\allowbreak param\_\allowbreak matched.py},
\texttt{utils.py}, \texttt{run\_\allowbreak atari.py}), and the CleanRL script
(a copy of which lives at this repository's root as
\texttt{ppo\_\allowbreak rnd\_\allowbreak envpool.py}).  All three share the
orthogonal-\(\sqrt{2}\), zero-bias initialization; they differ in predictor
depth, warmup, readout, and reward normalization.}
\label{tab:trainrun3-2-2-rnd-init}
\end{table}

\paragraph{One pipeline, three sets of modules.}
All three implementations compute the bonus through the same four-stage
pipeline; only the modules plugged into each stage differ.  Define \(x\) as
the raw input state, \((\mu, \sigma)\) as the running observation statistics
(filled by the warmup of Table~\ref{tab:trainrun3-2-2-rnd-init}), \(f\) as the
frozen target network with parameters \(\theta_f\) and \(g\) as the trained
predictor with parameters \(\theta_g\) --- both drawn at initialization from
the shared law \(\mathcal{I} = \{\text{orthogonal weights, gain }\sqrt{2};
\text{ biases } 0\}\) --- \(\rho\) as the bonus readout, and \(\nu\) as the
reward normalizer.  The pipeline is
\begin{align}
\bar{x} &= \mathrm{clip}\!\big((x - \mu)/\sigma,\, -5,\, 5\big)
&&\text{(whitening; all three)}\\
e(x) &= f(\bar{x};\theta_f) - g(\bar{x};\theta_g),
\qquad \theta_f, \theta_g \sim \mathcal{I}
&&\text{(residual; shared init law)}\\
b(x) &= \rho\big(e(x)\big),
\qquad \rho = \tfrac{1}{2}\lVert\cdot\rVert_2^2
\;\text{ or }\; \tfrac{1}{m}\lVert\cdot\rVert_2^2
&&\text{(readout; see table)}\\
r^{\mathrm{int}}(x) &= \nu\big(b(x)\big),
\qquad \nu = \beta\cdot\mathrm{id}
\;\text{ or }\; b / \mathrm{std}\big(\mathrm{filtered\ returns}\big)
&&\text{(normalizer; see table)}
\end{align}
where this work uses \(\rho = \frac{1}{2}\lVert\cdot\rVert_2^2\) (CleanRL the
same, the original the per-dimension mean) and \(\nu = \beta\cdot\mathrm{id}\)
(both references instead divide by the running standard deviation of
forward-filtered intrinsic returns).  The architectures inside \(f\) and
\(g\) differ per the table, but the initialization law \(\mathcal{I}\) is
identical everywhere --- and it is \(\mathcal{I}\)'s zero biases that shape
the initial field: a bias-free ReLU network is positively homogeneous
(\(f(a\bar{x}) = a\,f(\bar{x})\) for \(a>0\) in the two-layer case;
\citealp{zhang2024bias}), so both networks map the whitened origin to
zero, \(e \to 0\) there, and the initial bonus grows with
\(\lVert\bar{x}\rVert\) --- the bowl of
Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun3-2-2-initfield} is a direct consequence of the
initialization law all three implementations share.

\paragraph{Where the shared initialization law comes from.}
The law \(\mathcal{I}\) was never argued for --- it was inherited.  The RND
paper states no initialization scheme; its appendix defers entirely to the
released code (``exact details are given in the code accompanying this
paper''; \citealp{burda2019exploration}), and that code takes the
orthogonal-\(\sqrt{2}\), zero-bias initializer verbatim from the OpenAI
Baselines utilities shared by all of that codebase's PPO/A2C agents
\citep{baselines}.  The convention's stated justifications all concern
\emph{trainable} networks:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
\item orthogonal weights keep the layer-to-layer signal and the gradients
well-conditioned at any depth \citep{saxe2013exact};
\item the gain \(\sqrt{2}\) restores the variance that a ReLU halves
\citep{he2015delving};
\item in deep RL the choice is defended empirically --- orthogonal
initialization outperformed Xavier on final return
\citep{engstrom2019implementation}.
\end{itemize}
Neither argument applies to the frozen target \(f\): it is never trained, so
there is no gradient to condition, and the one scale problem the RND authors
do discuss --- a frozen network cannot adapt to the input scale --- they solve
by whitening the \emph{input}, not by choosing the weights.  The CleanRL port
copies the law without comment \citep{huang2022cleanrl}.  We found no
discussion of the choice, or of the initial-bonus field it produces, in the
paper, in the released repository and its issue tracker, or in any RND variant
since: the zero-bias line that creates the bowl of
Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun3-2-2-initfield} was carried along as codebase
convention, not made as a decision.

\paragraph{Does a nonzero bias initialization remove the bowl?  A controlled
ablation.}
If the bowl is really the zero-bias artifact, re-drawing the biases should
fill it --- by a predictable amount.  Write \(b(\bar{x})\) for the mse bonus
of the pipeline above as a function of the whitened input, define
\(r = \lVert\bar{x}\rVert\) as the distance from the whitening center, and
let every bias entry of both networks (both layers) be drawn i.i.d.\
\(\mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)\).  At the whitened origin the weight paths
contribute nothing, so the residual there is built from the biases alone, and
the second-moment identities of the orthogonal gain-\(\sqrt{2}\) law give
\begin{align}
\mathbb{E}\big[b(0)\big] = 256\,\sigma^2,
\qquad
\mathbb{E}\big[b(\bar{x})\big] \approx r^2 + 256\,\sigma^2
\end{align}
(each network contributes \(128\sigma^2\) through its output-layer biases and
\(128\sigma^2\) through its hidden biases passed through the output weights):
a bias raises the whole field by a \emph{constant floor} and leaves the
quadratic growth in \(r\) intact.  The ablation holds everything else fixed
--- the step-0 weights and the observation normalizer of the
Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun3-2-2-initfield} network are byte-identical across
arms --- and overwrites only the biases: the zero baseline; PyTorch's own
default \(\mathcal{U}(-1/\sqrt{\text{fan-in}}, +1/\sqrt{\text{fan-in}})\),
i.e.\ what the networks would get if \texttt{layer\_init} simply did not zero
the biases; and \(\mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)\) at
\(\sigma \in \{0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 1, 2\}\), the same unit draw rescaled so the
sweep varies only the scale.  Metrics are computed on the free-space points of
the dense \(100\times100\) grid at \(v{=}0\) for seeds \(\{111, 0, 1, 2, 3\}\):
the max/min ratio, the bonus at the free point nearest the whitening center
(\(\lVert\bar{x}\rVert \approx 0.09\)), the Spearman rank correlation
\(\rho(b, r)\), and the coefficient of variation
\(\mathrm{CV} = \mathrm{std}/\mathrm{mean}\).
Table~\ref{tab:trainrun3-2-2-bias-ablation} and
Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun3-2-2-bias-ablation} give the result:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{The pit is the zero-bias artifact.}  With zero biases the
free-space max/min ratio at \(v{=}0\) is \(2864 \pm 1554\) (mean \(\pm\) sd
over the five seeds; \(4442\) at seed \(111\)) --- far beyond the
\(>100\times\) read off Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun3-2-2-initfield}, because the
dense grid samples much closer to the field's true minimum than the
\(10\times10\) midpoints do.  Every nonzero bias of realistic size removes
two to three orders of magnitude of that spread; the PyTorch default alone
brings the ratio to \(5.2\).
\item \textbf{The bias must be comparable to the first-layer signal.}
\(\sigma{=}0.1\) barely helps (ratio \(17.9\); rank correlation with \(r\)
unchanged at \(0.96\)).  The PyTorch default works as well as
\(\sigma \approx 0.3\) because the first layer's fan-in is \(4\), so its
default bias bound is \(1/\sqrt{4} = 0.5\).
\item \textbf{Bias lifts the floor, not the shape.}  The measured center
bonus matches the \(256\sigma^2\) prediction within \(2.5\%\) at every
\(\sigma\) (Table~\ref{tab:trainrun3-2-2-bias-ablation}), and the rank
correlation with \(r\) stays at \(0.86\)--\(0.96\) through
\(\sigma \le 0.5\): the outward growth survives, as
Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun3-2-2-bias-ablation}(b) shows directly.  The
near-perfect flatness at \(\sigma \ge 1\) is degenerate: there the constant
\(256\sigma^2\) drowns the \(r^2\) term, giving a nearly state-independent
bonus that distinguishes nothing (it only rescales the effective \(\beta\)),
and biases beyond the first layer's per-unit signal scale
(\(\approx 0.09\,r\), i.e.\ \(\approx 0.5\) at the far edge of the position
field) pin ReLU units on or off across the whole input box.
\item \textbf{Recommendation.}  \(\sigma \in [0.25, 0.5]\) is the useful
window (ratio \(3.8\) down to \(1.7\) with the network still genuinely
nonlinear); the cheapest implementation is deleting the line that zeroes the
biases in \texttt{layer\_init}, which leaves PyTorch's default and already
yields a ratio of \(\approx 5\).  Bias initialization alone, however, removes
the pit and not the hill: a genuinely even start needs the field-level
remedies of the list below (subtracting the initial field, or bounding or
normalizing the network output).
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[H]

  persists.}
\end{subfigure}
\caption{The controlled bias-initialization ablation of
Table~\ref{tab:trainrun3-2-2-bias-ablation}.  In (a) the deep central pit of
the zero-bias field fills in as the bias scale grows, until at
\(\sigma \ge 1\) the field is nearly constant; in (b) the same data as bonus
vs.\ radius shows that the biases add a floor while the slope of the
\(r^2\) growth is unchanged.}
\label{fig:trainrun3-2-2-bias-ablation}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Making the initial bonus more even.}
Literature-supported remedies, ordered from most directly on-point:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Distill several targets instead of one.}  Distributional RND
\citep{yang2024exploration} names exactly the problem measured here (``initial bonus
inconsistency: the uneven distribution of bonuses among states at the
beginning of training'') and replaces the single frozen target with \(N\)
random targets whose \emph{mean} is distilled; averaging shrinks the initial
field's variance.  Random Distribution Distillation \citep{fang2025exploration} goes
further and parameterizes the target's output \emph{distribution}.  This
project's \texttt{n\_predictors} ensemble switch is the predictor-side analog.
\item \textbf{Ensembles with randomized priors.}  Ensemble-disagreement
bonuses over fixed random prior networks \citep{osband2018randomized} are
provably conservative and concentrate with data
\citep{ciosek2019conservative}; anti-concentrated confidence bonuses
\citep{ashanti} use an ensemble of noise-predicting regressors
to approximate the elliptical bonus at scale.  More members \(\Rightarrow\) a
smoother, better-calibrated initial field than one predictor--target pair.
\item \textbf{Break the zero-bias homogeneity at the source.}  Non-zero bias
initialization, a bounded target output (a final \(\tanh\) or layer
normalization on \(f\)), or unit-normalizing the input feature (as this
project's elliptical bonus already does) all remove the
\(\lVert\bar{x}\rVert\)-dependence that creates the bowl
\citep[mechanism per][]{zhang2024bias}.  To our knowledge no published RND
variant adopts this specifically to even the initial bonus.  The
bias-initialization half of this remedy is tested directly above
(Table~\ref{tab:trainrun3-2-2-bias-ablation},
Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun3-2-2-bias-ablation}): it removes the pit but not the
radial growth, so it is a partial fix on its own.
\item \textbf{Subtract the initial field (learning-progress reading).}  The
step-0 bonus \(b_0(x)\) is a pure initialization artifact, so rewarding
\(b(x) - b_0(x)\) (or, more generally, the \emph{novelty of the surprise},
\citealp{le2023beyond}) starts exactly even at zero; \(b_0\) can be
cached once at launch for negligible cost.
\item \textbf{Bound and normalize the raw signal (scale, not shape).}  The
original recipe --- whitening, the \(\pm5\) clip, and dividing by the running
std of filtered intrinsic returns \citep{burda2019exploration} --- and NGU's
modulator clipped to \([1, L]\) \citep{badia2020never} control the bonus
\emph{scale} and outliers, but the spatial bowl survives them (as the
measurement above shows: our field is whitened and clipped, and still spans
two orders of magnitude).
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Train run 3.2.3: nonzero bias initialization and classic reward normalization}
\label{sec:train-run-3-2-3}

\paragraph{Run.}
Train run~3.2.3 turns two remedies from the list above into training sweeps:
breaking the zero-bias homogeneity at the source (the third remedy, whose
untrained-field effect the bias ablation measured directly), and the original
recipe's scale normalization (the fifth remedy, which this codebase had never
actually enabled).  Everything else is the run-3.2.1 stack unchanged: SAC on
\texttt{PointMaze\_\allowbreak Large-v3}, the \texttt{rnd\_\allowbreak next\_\allowbreak state}
input, \(10^{6}\) steps, and the training-episode reward as the score.  Three
arms:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Reward-norm.}  The benchmark configuration ---
Adam at PyTorch defaults, \(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{mse}}\) readout, zero biases
--- with the classic intrinsic-reward normalization switched on
(\texttt{rnd\_\allowbreak reward\_\allowbreak norm}, next paragraph), sweeping
\(\beta\in\{10^{-4},\dots,10^{4}\}\) (9 decades; one decade lower than the
run-3.2.1 grid because the normalized bonus is rescaled to unit return-std,
so useful \(\beta\) values shift down).
\item \textbf{Adam\(\times\)init (nonzero biases).}  Adam with the
\(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{l2}}\) readout only (user decision at launch,
2026-07-11), the two bias-initialization schemes below, and
\(\beta\in\{10^{-5},\dots,10^{4}\}\) (10 decades; extended one further decade
down because a nonzero bias raises the initial bonus floor by up to two orders
of magnitude, Table~\ref{tab:trainrun3-2-2-bias-ablation}).
\item \textbf{SGD-\(1/t\)\(\times\)init (nonzero biases).}  The run-3.2.1
SGD-\(1/t\) family with the same readout, schemes, and \(\beta\) grid as
Adam\(\times\)init, with the schedule grid trimmed to the region run 3.2.1
found competitive: \(\eta_0\in\{10^{-3},10^{-2},10^{-1}\}\),
\(t_0\in\{10^{3},10^{4}\}\).
\end{itemize}
The two remedies are deliberately not crossed: the reward-norm arm keeps zero biases, and
the two init arms keep the normalization off, so each effect is measured
against the same unmodified benchmark.  The reference is the RND benchmark
re-measured on fresh seeds in run~3.2.2
(Table~\ref{tab:trainrun3-2-2-validation}): \(\bar{R}=34.50\),
\(\mathrm{SE}=3.13\), \(n=100\).  It is not re-run here.

\paragraph{The reward normalization (the reward-norm arm).}
This codebase has never normalized the intrinsic reward: the raw readout
enters the replay reward \(r^{\mathrm{ext}} + \beta\,r^{\mathrm{int}}\)
directly, and only the RND \emph{input} is whitened
(\texttt{rnd\_\allowbreak obs\_\allowbreak norm}).  The reward-norm arm adds the original
paper's reward-side normalization \citep{burda2019exploration}.  Define
\(r^{\mathrm{int}}_t\) as the raw bonus readout on the transition collected at
environment step \(t\), \(\gamma_{\mathrm{norm}}\) as the filter discount
(\texttt{rnd\_\allowbreak reward\_\allowbreak norm\_\allowbreak gamma}
\(=0.99\), the paper's intrinsic discount), \(F_t\) as the forward-filtered
intrinsic return (one accumulator per environment copy, not reset at episode
boundaries; \(F_0=r^{\mathrm{int}}_0\)), and \(\sigma_F\) as the running
standard deviation of all filter values so far.  The filter and the normalized
reward are
\begin{align}
F_t = \gamma_{\mathrm{norm}}\,F_{t-1} + r^{\mathrm{int}}_t,
\qquad
\hat{r}^{\mathrm{int}}_t = r^{\mathrm{int}}_t / \sigma_F .
\end{align}
Placement in this off-policy stack: the statistics \((F_t,\sigma_F)\) advance
once per \emph{collected} transition, in time order, through the replay
buffer's add-time hook; the division by the current \(\sigma_F\) applies
wherever the bonus is read --- the replay-sampled training reward and the
logged per-step intrinsic --- while evaluation rollouts, which only read the
bonus, never move the statistics.  The predictor's training loss is
unnormalized (the paper normalizes the reward, not the distillation
objective).

\paragraph{The bias initialization (arms Adam\(\times\)init, SGD-\(1/t\)\(\times\)init).}
The bias ablation above (Table~\ref{tab:trainrun3-2-2-bias-ablation},
Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun3-2-2-bias-ablation}) measured the untrained field;
these arms measure whether the flattened start actually helps training.  The
switch (\texttt{rnd\_\allowbreak bias\_\allowbreak init}) overwrites the biases
of both nets' two Linear layers --- the orthogonal weights are never touched
--- with the ablation's own keyed draws, so a training run's initial bonus
field \emph{equals} the ablation's measured field for the same seed.  The
two schemes span the ablation's flatness range: \texttt{pytorch\_\allowbreak
default} (the framework's own bias law
\(\mathcal{U}(-1/\sqrt{\mathrm{fan\_in}},+1/\sqrt{\mathrm{fan\_in}})\);
free-point max/min ratio \(\approx 5.2\)) and \texttt{normal\_\allowbreak 0.5}
(\(\mathcal{N}(0,0.5^{2})\); ratio \(\approx 1.7\), versus \(\approx 2{,}900\)
for the zero-bias baseline).

\paragraph{Seed allocation and pruning (as run 3.2.1, new bar and cap).}
The sweep runs under the same adaptive controller as run~3.2.1
(Section~\ref{sec:train-run-3-2-1}): every configuration races on the shared
seed list, and the controller stops a configuration once its data rule out the
bar.  Two parameters change.  First, the bar is now the \runref{run-3.2.2 benchmark}'s fresh-seed mean
\(34.50\) (frozen at first controller start by recomputing it from run-3.2.2's
benchmark records) rather than the run-3.1.1 in-sample mean.  Second, the seed cap is
\(100\) (\texttt{a\_seed} \(0\)--\(99\)), doubling run-3.2.1's cap so surviving
configurations end with validation-grade precision.  Recall the rule: with
\(n\) finished seeds of mean \(\bar{R}\) and sample standard deviation \(s\), a
configuration is stopped when \(n\ge 10\) and
\(\bar{R} + 2.576\,s/\sqrt{n} < 34.50\) (the one-sided \(99\%\) upper limit
sits below the benchmark mean).  The grid is
reward-norm\,(9) \(+\) Adam\(\times\)init\,(\(2\times10=20\)) \(+\)
SGD-\(1/t\)\(\times\)init\,(\(2\times3\times2\times10=120\)) \(=149\)
configurations \(\times\) the 100-seed cap \(=14{,}900\) queue entries;
execution follows the seed-outermost id order, so early seeds finish first and
stopped configurations end near the 10-seed floor.

\paragraph{Results.}
The sweep completed on 2026-07-18: all \(149\) configurations are decided —
\(132\) stopped by the bar rule and \(17\) ran to the \(100\)-seed cap — so
\(10{,}240\) of the \(14{,}900\) queued runs never executed.  Notably, \(8\) of
the \(9\) reward-normalization configurations were pruned; the lone survivor
(\(\beta{=}10^{4}\), the top of its grid, as the unit-return rescaling
predicts) leads the whole sweep.  Table~\ref{tab:trainrun3-2-3-results}
reports the best configuration (at least \(10\) seeds) per arm and bias
scheme; recall \(\mathrm{succ.}\) is the fraction of seeds with final training
reward above \(5\) (Section~\ref{sec:train-run-3-2-1}).
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Reward normalization leads in-sample.}  The reward-norm arm at \(\beta{=}10^{4}\)
reaches \(\bar{R}=45.6\) with the highest success rate of the table
(\(0.92\)) — about \(11\) points above the \runref{run-3.2.2 benchmark}.
\item \textbf{Nonzero biases lift SGD-\(1/t\) again.}  Both SGD-\(1/t\)\(\times\)init rows clear the
benchmark (\(43.8\) with \texttt{normal\_0.5}, \(41.4\) with
\texttt{pytorch\_\allowbreak default}), echoing run 3.2.1's SGD-\(1/t\) lead
— which run 3.2.2 then showed to be selection bias, hence the fresh-seed
validation below.
\item \textbf{Adam gains little from a nonzero bias.}  Adam\(\times\)init with
\texttt{normal\_0.5} sits just above the benchmark (\(35.5\)); with
\texttt{pytorch\_\allowbreak default} it stays below (\(31.8\)).
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Train run 3.2.4: fresh-seed validation of the run-3.2.3 top five}
\label{sec:train-run-3-2-4}

\paragraph{Run.}
The run-3.2.3 winners are again an argmax over many noisy cells, so — exactly
as run 3.2.2 did for run 3.2.1 — the top five racing configurations by
in-sample mean at selection time (2026-07-14) were re-run, numbered rank~1--rank~5
by that in-sample mean, on \(100\)
\emph{fresh} seeds (\texttt{a\_seed} \(500\)--\(599\), disjoint from the
\(0\)--\(499\) used by every prior run), with no pruning: every configuration
runs all \(100\) seeds.  Same stack and metric as run 3.2.3; jobs on the open
cpu partition, nolim, and the reservation nodes.  Two rank-1 seeds (\(575\),
\(598\)) diverged within minutes of starting — the SAC actor emitted NaN
under the largest initial bonus of the five — and are excluded from \(n\) and
reported here rather than requeued (the failure is the model's, not the
infrastructure's, and reproduces deterministically from the seed).

\paragraph{Results (partial snapshot 2026-07-18, \(n\approx 75\) of 100).}
Recall from Section~\ref{sec:train-run-3-2-2} the gap
\(\Delta=\bar{R}_c-\bar{R}_b\) against the benchmark and the one-sided
confidence \(\Phi\!\big(\Delta/\sqrt{\mathrm{SE}_c^2+\mathrm{SE}_b^2}\big)\)
that a candidate exceeds it.  Table~\ref{tab:trainrun3-2-4-validation} reports
the five candidates on the fresh seeds.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Two configurations validate out-of-sample.}  Rank~1
(SGD-\(1/t\)/l2/\(\beta{=}1\), \texttt{normal\_0.5}) holds at
\(\bar{R}=49.3\) (\(\Delta=+14.8\), confidence \(100\%\)) and rank~3 (the
reward-normalized Adam at \(\beta{=}10^{4}\)) at \(44.7\)
(\(\Delta=+10.2\), \(98\%\)) — the first configurations in this project to
beat the RND benchmark on fresh seeds.
\item \textbf{The winner's curse strikes again, selectively.}  Rank~2 — second
in-sample at selection (\(41.9\)) — collapses to \(29.0\) on fresh seeds
(\(\Delta=-5.5\)), the same selection-bias regression run 3.2.2 measured for
the run-3.2.1 winners; ranks~4 and~5 land near the benchmark.
\item \textbf{Caveat.}  About \(75\) of \(100\) seeds per configuration are
finished at this snapshot; the remaining runs execute on a final worker wave
(part of the tail was restarted after a job-walltime kill), and the table is
regenerated at completion — the numbers move a little, not qualitatively.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Train run 4 (fully-JAX, sample-time reproduction of run 2)}
\label{sec:train-run-4}

\paragraph{Purpose.}
Train run~4 reproduces Train run~2 with the SAC agent and the \texttt{rnd\_\allowbreak state} intrinsic moved
entirely into JAX: \texttt{sbx} provides the XLA-jitted SAC, and a flax/optax RND predictor,
target, and running observation normalization (\texttt{jax\_\allowbreak rnd.JaxRND}) replace the torch RND, so
the per-step intrinsic path contains no torch. The aim is twofold --- confirm the JAX implementation
reproduces run-2's learning curves, and measure the end-to-end speedup. The algorithm is
identical to run~2; the only intended difference is $5\times10^{5}$ training steps (run~2
used $10^{6}$). In particular the intrinsic reward is recomputed at \textbf{sample time} --- on each sampled
gradient-step batch, exactly as run-2's \texttt{VectorIntrinsicReplayBuffer} does, not when a state is first
visited. The SAC subclass \texttt{SACWithIntrinsic} performs this recompute at \texttt{sbx}'s numpy hook
before the jitted update: the buffer stores the extrinsic reward, and $r=r^{\mathrm{ext}}+\beta\,r^{\mathrm{int}}$
is rebuilt on the sampled batch every gradient step with the predictor trained then. Table~\ref{tab:trainrun4-parity}
lists every hyperparameter against run~2; only a few values differ.

\paragraph{Logging.}
Run~4 adopts the revised logging convention (Section~\ref{sec:logging}): besides \texttt{eval\_\allowbreak history}
and \texttt{train\_\allowbreak history}, every run saves \texttt{train\_\allowbreak episode\_\allowbreak
history} --- one record per completed training episode (episode-level, not step-level, to save space) --- so the
full first-to-last training-episode trajectory is retained. The eval-at-final-parameters special case is
removed: the final evaluation uses the same \texttt{n\_\allowbreak eval\_\allowbreak episodes}
($=100$) as every other evaluation. Run~4 logs the reward groups (\texttt{eval\_\allowbreak history} with the
\texttt{visit\_\allowbreak counts/}$*$ coverage block, \texttt{train\_\allowbreak history}, and
\texttt{train\_\allowbreak episode\_\allowbreak history}) but \textbf{not} the oracle-distance group
(\texttt{distance\_\allowbreak history}): \texttt{log\_\allowbreak distance} is off by default, since run~4 is a
reward comparison and run~2's data already carries the distance-to-ground-truth field. Each sweep's JSONs land
under \texttt{data/<sweep\_\allowbreak id>/local/} (the sweep-id convention), so a rerun extends coverage
without colliding with earlier data.

\paragraph{Comparison and finding.}
Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun4-comparison} overlays the eval extrinsic reward over training for run~2
(\textbf{dashed}) and run~4 (\textbf{solid}), one color per algorithm, together with run~4's
training-evaluation curve (a \textbf{dash-dot} line of the same color) --- the mean extrinsic reward over the
past \texttt{n\_\allowbreak eval\_\allowbreak episodes} completed \emph{training} episodes at each evaluation
step. Each line is drawn only up to the largest step reached by at least $30$ seeds.
\textbf{Run~4 does not reproduce run~2.} The curves match up to $\approx2.5\times10^{5}$ steps, after which
run~2 keeps climbing while run~4 grows slowly; at $5\times10^{5}$ steps run~4 reaches only
$0.39$--$0.52\times$ run~2's reward for every algorithm (Table~\ref{tab:trainrun4-result}). The shortfall is
attributable to the \textbf{SAC backend}, not the intrinsic path or the sample-time recompute:
\texttt{gt\_\allowbreak position\_\allowbreak velocity} uses the \emph{identical} torch visit-count bonus with
the same sample-time recompute as run~2 and differs only in the agent (\texttt{sbx}/JAX SAC vs
Stable-Baselines3/torch SAC), yet shows the same gap. So swapping the SAC implementation to \texttt{sbx} is
\emph{not behavior-preserving} on this sparse-reward task --- even with every audited hyperparameter matched
(Table~\ref{tab:trainrun4-parity}) --- and the divergence appears in the late-training exploitation phase,
after the exploration bonus has largely decayed. Run~4 is, however, \textbf{$1.55$--$1.81\times$ faster} per
run at matched step count ($\approx2.9$--$3.1$\,h vs $\approx4.7$--$5.2$\,h for $5\times10^{5}$ steps on $2$
CPUs; below the $\sim2.2\times$ an isolated single-run benchmark suggested, because the sweep packs $8$ workers
per node on slower, more contended hardware). The sweep was stopped at $395/600$ runs once the gap was
established. \textbf{Takeaway:} folding the RND predictor into JAX is correct and faster, but reproducing
run~2 in JAX would require matching \texttt{sbx}'s SAC to Stable-Baselines3's, which this run shows is not
automatic.

\caption{Train run~4 (fully-JAX, sample-time) vs Train run~2 (torch): eval extrinsic reward over training,

$5\times10^{5}$ steps); \textbf{dash-dot} = run~4 training-evaluation (past-$100$-episode training mean), same color.
Bands are $\pm$ standard error over seeds; each line stops at the last step reached by $\geq 30$ seeds.
Run-4 (solid) falls below run-2 (dashed) after $\approx2.5\times10^{5}$ steps --- see Table~\ref{tab:trainrun4-result}.}
\label{fig:trainrun4-comparison}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Train run 5 (set baseline: original RND)}
\label{sec:train-run-5}

\paragraph{Run.}
Train run~5 fixes a clean baseline: three RND configurations, each on $300$ fresh seeds
(\texttt{a\_\allowbreak seed} $600$--$899$, disjoint from the $0$--$599$ used by every prior run),
same SAC / \texttt{PointMaze\_\allowbreak Large-v3} stack and training-episode metric as run~3.2.4.
The three arms are:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=*]
\item \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} --- the \emph{original} RND recipe \citep{burda2019exploration}
  transplanted onto this task on the train-run-3 RND network size, \sout{swept over eight intrinsic
  weights that race under a pruning controller}\claudeedit{run at each of the eight intrinsic
  weights $\beta \in \{10^{-2}, 10^{-1}, 0.5, 1, 10, 10^{2}, 10^{3}, 10^{4}\}$, which race against
  each other under a truncation controller};
\item \runref{run-3.2.2 benchmark} --- the RND benchmark of run 3.2.2 (Adam, $\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{mse}}$, $\beta{=}100$,
  no reward normalization), re-run at $300$ seeds; and
\item \runref{run-3.2.3 reward-norm} --- the reward-normalized winner of run 3.2.3 (Adam, $\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{mse}}$,
  $\beta{=}10^{4}$, reward normalization $\gamma_{\mathrm{norm}}{=}0.99$), re-run at $300$ seeds.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Purpose.}
Two goals. First, the project has always compared its bonuses against its own ``RND benchmark''
(the \runref{run-3.2.2 benchmark} line), never against a faithful \emph{original} RND; \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} is that reference,
assembling the scattered original-RND facts of Section~\ref{sec:rnd-norm} and
Table~\ref{tab:rnd-norm} / Table~\ref{tab:trainrun3-2-2-rnd-init} into one run. Second, the benchmark and reward-norm configurations
already have fresh-seed data (runs 3.2.2/3.2.3/3.2.4); re-running them at $300$ seeds lets us test,
per previous run, whether the new data differs enough to stand alone or whether the old data may be
pooled in --- the substitution check under \emph{Results}.

\paragraph{Original RND, component by component.}
Before the translation we restate the original recipe one component at a time, each time giving the
original \citep{burda2019exploration} value, then CleanRL's \citep{huang2022cleanrl}, then --- where
it is informative --- ExPLORe's \citep{li2023accelerating} state-input RND. The two Atari references
run no continuous-control task (verified: the original repository and paper evaluate only Atari plus
an MNIST toy, and CleanRL's only RND is the Atari envpool script), so ExPLORe supplies the
state/continuous reference. The full cell-by-cell values are in Table~\ref{tab:trainrun5-hparams}.

\paragraph{State normalization.}
The original whitens the RND input by a running mean/std with a clip to $[-5,5]$ (no denominator
$\epsilon$), and seeds those statistics from a random agent before training: $128$ parallel copies
$\times\,50$ steps $=6400$ warm-up transitions.
Recall from Section~\ref{sec:rnd-norm-overview} that the tracker maintains
$(\mu,\sigma^{2},n)$ --- the running per-dimension mean and variance estimates and the count of
values seen so far --- initialized at $\mu=0$, $\sigma^{2}=1$, $n=10^{-4}$, and that $x$ is the RND
input. Each incoming batch of RND inputs, with per-dimension mean $m_B$, variance $v_B$, and size
$n_B$, updates the estimates to $(\mu',\sigma'^{2})$ by the pooled-moments rule of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:rms-update},
\begin{align}
n' &= n + n_B,
\qquad
\delta = m_B - \mu,
\qquad
\mu' = \mu + \delta\,\frac{n_B}{n'},\\
\sigma'^{2} &= \frac{n\,\sigma^{2} + n_B\,v_B}{n'} + \delta^{2}\,\frac{n\,n_B}{n'^{2}},
\end{align}
and the original's whitening is then Eq.~\eqref{eq:obs-norm} with no denominator constant,
\begin{align}
\tilde{x} = \mathrm{clip}\!\big((x-\mu)/\sqrt{\sigma^{2}},\,-5,\,5\big),
\end{align}
while this project's translation divides by $\sqrt{\sigma^{2}+10^{-8}}$ (the obs-norm denominator
of Table~\ref{tab:trainrun5-hparams}).
CleanRL is identical except it excludes the initial
reset observations from the warm-up. ExPLORe applies \emph{no} state normalization at all --- the
predictor and target consume the raw state-action vector.

\paragraph{Intrinsic reward normalization.}
Define $r^{\mathrm{int}}_t$ as the raw bonus on the transition collected at step $t$,
$\gamma_{\mathrm{norm}}$ as the intrinsic discount ($=0.99$), $F_t$ as the forward-filtered
intrinsic return (one accumulator per environment copy, not reset at episode boundaries;
$F_0=r^{\mathrm{int}}_0$),
and $\sigma_F$ as the running standard deviation of all filter values so far. The original filter
and normalized reward are
\begin{align}
F_t = \gamma_{\mathrm{norm}}\,F_{t-1} + r^{\mathrm{int}}_t,
\qquad
\hat{r}^{\mathrm{int}}_t = r^{\mathrm{int}}_t / \sigma_F ,
\end{align}
with no mean subtracted, so the bonus stays non-negative. CleanRL implements the same filter and
running-std division with $\gamma_{\mathrm{norm}}{=}0.99$. ExPLORe uses \emph{no} reward
normalization --- its bonus is the raw distillation error times a constant coefficient
($\mathrm{coeff}{=}1.0$).

\paragraph{Target and predictor architecture.}
The original target is a three-layer convolutional trunk (LeakyReLU) followed by a single linear
projection to $m{=}512$; the predictor is a \emph{separate} network of the same trunk architecture
(independently initialized, no weight sharing) that adds \emph{two} extra $512$-wide ReLU layers
before its $512$-output --- the predictor is deliberately \emph{deeper} than the target.
Both use orthogonal initialization with gain $\sqrt2$ and zero biases. CleanRL matches this shape
(its convolutional LeakyReLU slope is the PyTorch default $0.01$ rather than the original's $0.2$).
ExPLORe departs sharply: a state-action MLP with hidden widths $(256,256,256)$ and a $256$-feature
head, Xavier/Glorot initialization, and predictor \emph{identical} to the target (symmetric, no
extra depth).

\paragraph{Bonus readout.}
Let $e\in\mathbb{R}^m$ be the per-dimension error between target and predictor features. The
original reads out the bonus as the \emph{mean} over dimensions,
$\tfrac1m\sum_j e_j^2$ (no $\tfrac12$ factor), and trains the predictor with the same reduction.
CleanRL instead reports the bonus as $\tfrac12\sum_j e_j^2$ (a sum, halved) while --- inconsistently
--- taking the mean over dimensions in the predictor \emph{loss}. ExPLORe uses the same
mean-over-dimensions readout as the original paper (times its constant coefficient).

\paragraph{Predictor optimizer and update proportion.}
The original trains the predictor with Adam at learning rate $10^{-4}$ (betas $(0.9,0.999)$,
$\epsilon{=}10^{-8}$), inside one joint optimizer that also carries the policy and value networks,
with no gradient clipping. CleanRL is the same except $\epsilon{=}10^{-5}$ and a global-norm
gradient clip of $0.5$. On the fraction of each batch used to train the predictor there is a
documentation-vs-code split worth stating plainly: the RND paper's Table~5 lists an update
proportion of $0.25$, and CleanRL uses $0.25$, but the \emph{original repository's own code}
defaults to $1.0$ (train on the whole batch). ExPLORe takes one Adam step ($3\times10^{-4}$) per
environment step on a single fresh transition, with no subsampling.

\paragraph{The RL side.}
The original and CleanRL are PPO with \emph{two} value heads: an intrinsic head trained on
non-episodic returns (intrinsic discount $\gamma_{\mathrm{int}}{=}0.99$) and an extrinsic head on
episodic returns (extrinsic discount $\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}$; the original code uses $0.99$, its
paper and CleanRL use $0.999$). The two advantages are combined with an extrinsic coefficient of
$2$ and an intrinsic coefficient of $1$ --- a $2\!:\!1$ extrinsic-to-intrinsic weighting, run with
$128$ parallel environments. ExPLORe instead runs RLPD (SAC with a $50/50$ offline/online replay
mix, UTD $20$, one environment) and \emph{adds} the bonus directly to the scalar reward with no
separate value head. The project's own RND (train run 3) and run~5 likewise use SAC with a single
reward stream $r^{\mathrm{ext}}+\beta\,r^{\mathrm{int}}$.

\paragraph{From Atari-PPO to PointMaze-SAC: the translation choices.}
The \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} arm keeps the original RND settings above wherever the SAC/PointMaze/state setting allows,
and makes the following explicit translations:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{CNN $\to$ MLP.} The $84\times84$ convolutional trunk becomes a fully-connected trunk
  on the $4$-dimensional state $(x,y,v_x,v_y)$.
\item \textbf{Small widths, deeper predictor.} The \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} arm takes the train-run-3 RND \emph{widths}
  ($256$ hidden, output $m{=}128$) but keeps the original's defining \emph{asymmetry}: the predictor
  is one $128$-wide LeakyReLU$(0.2)$ block deeper than the target. Target
  $=\;$state$(4)\!\to\!256\!\to\!\mathrm{LReLU}\!\to\!128$; predictor
  $=\;$state$(4)\!\to\!256\!\to\!\mathrm{LReLU}\!\to\!128\!\to\!\mathrm{LReLU}\!\to\!128$ (output
  dim stays $128$ so the distillation error dimension matches the target).
\item \textbf{Activation.} LeakyReLU with negative slope $0.2$ --- the original's
  \texttt{tf.nn.\allowbreak leaky\_\allowbreak relu} default, \emph{not} PyTorch's $0.01$ (which is
  what CleanRL inherits).
\item \textbf{Readout.} the original mean-over-dimensions $\tfrac1m\sum_j e_j^2$
  (\texttt{mse\_\allowbreak mean}).
\item \textbf{Optimizer.} Adam at $10^{-4}$, $\epsilon{=}10^{-8}$ (the original's value).
\item \textbf{Update proportion $1.0$.} following the original \emph{repository code} default (train
  on the whole replay batch), not the paper's Table-5 value of $0.25$.
\item \textbf{Warm-up.} $6400$ random-agent environment steps (the original's count), rolled on a
  fresh throwaway environment so the training visit counts are untouched.
\item \textbf{Reward normalization.} on, $\gamma_{\mathrm{norm}}{=}0.99$.
\item \textbf{Single reward stream.} SAC forms $r^{\mathrm{ext}}+\beta\,r^{\mathrm{int}}$ in the
  replay buffer; there is no separate intrinsic value head, so the $2\!:\!1$ advantage weighting
  becomes a single scalar $\beta$. The point $\beta{=}0.5$ is the faithful counterpart of the
  original's $2\!:\!1$ extrinsic-to-intrinsic ratio.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{\sout{Beta sweep and pruning.}\claudeedit{The eight bonus weights, and how a weight is
stopped early.}}
The \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} arm \sout{sweeps}\claudeedit{runs at each of the eight bonus
weights} $\beta\in\{10^{-2},10^{-1},0.5,1,10,10^{2},10^{3},10^{4}\}$. The eight \sout{betas}\claudeedit{weights}
race: let $\bar R_\beta$, $s_\beta$, and $n_\beta$ be the mean, sample standard deviation, and count
of a \sout{beta}\claudeedit{weight}'s finished-seed final training-episode rewards, and let the \emph{bar} be the largest
$\bar R_\beta$ over \sout{betas}\claudeedit{weights} with $n_\beta\ge 30$. A \sout{beta}\claudeedit{weight} is stopped once
\begin{align}
n_\beta \ge 30
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\bar R_\beta + 2.576\,\frac{s_\beta}{\sqrt{n_\beta}} \;<\; \mathrm{bar}
\end{align}
(its one-sided $99\%$ upper confidence limit sits below the running best mean); survivors run to the
full $300$ seeds. The benchmark and reward-norm arms never prune and run all $300$ seeds.

\claudeedit{\paragraph{The learning-rate addendum (submitted 2026-08-05, widened 2026-08-06).}
The \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} arm above follows the original paper's predictor learning rate of
$10^{-4}$. The last column of Table~\ref{tab:trainrun5-hparams} is the same stack at $10^{-3}$ and
at $10^{-2}$ (every row of that column reads \emph{same} except the optimizer, so the two rates
share one column) ---
one knob moved, every other field of train run 5's own parameter dictionary copied unchanged and
machine-checked to be unchanged. It runs on the same \texttt{PointMaze\_\allowbreak Large-v3}
top-right task, for the same $10^{6}$ env steps, on $100$ fresh seeds
(\texttt{a\_\allowbreak seed} $900$--$999$, disjoint from the $600$--$899$ above and from every
earlier PointMaze run). It runs at each of the $15$ bonus weights $\beta \in \{10^{-3},
3{\times}10^{-3}, 10^{-2}, 3{\times}10^{-2}, 10^{-1}, 3{\times}10^{-1}, 1, 3, 10, 30, 10^{2},
3{\times}10^{2}, 10^{3}, 3{\times}10^{3}, 10^{4}\}$ --- the grid of
Table~\ref{tab:pm-am-trainrun1-algos} rather than the eight values above, so the same range is swept
on this environment and on the two AntMaze environments of
subsubsection~\ref{sec:pm-am-trainrun12}.
The truncation rule differs from the one above in what it compares against. Define
$\bar R^{\star}$ as the mean final training-episode reward of the best $10^{-4}$ bonus weight ---
$\beta=10^{3}$, $\bar R^{\star} = 38.6412$ over its $n=300$ completed seeds, frozen before launch
and never recomputed. A $10^{-3}$ bonus weight with $n \ge 30$ completed seeds is stopped at the
first controller tick at which
\begin{align}
\bar R_\beta + 2.576\,\frac{s_\beta}{\sqrt{n_\beta}} \;<\; \bar R^{\star},
\end{align}
that is, when its one-sided $99\%$ upper confidence limit falls below what the $10^{-4}$ line
already achieved; a bonus weight that reaches the seed target without ever failing that check
is kept. So the addendum is not raced against itself: it is raced against the number it is meant to
beat. The second learning rate, $10^{-2}$, races against the same frozen bars under the same rule,
and both rates run the same seeds, so they are paired seed by seed with each other as well as
comparable to $10^{-4}$. The seed target was raised from $100$ to $300$ on 2026-08-06, at the same
time as the $10^{-2}$ rate joined.}
\claudecomment{Bar provenance:
\texttt{slurm/\allowbreak FROZEN\_\allowbreak BARS.json} in the addendum run folder, recomputed at
launch from train run 5's own records
(\texttt{data/\allowbreak 2026-07-20-16-55\_\allowbreak set-baseline/\allowbreak local}, $1{,}913$
JSONs) by \texttt{slurm/\allowbreak compute\_\allowbreak frozen\_\allowbreak bars.py}, which refuses
to write unless the recomputed winning bonus weight is the expected $10^{3}$. Arithmetic: mean
$38.6412$, standard deviation $30.7210$, $n=300$. Note this is a LATER snapshot than the $39.92$ at
$n=226$ quoted in the Results paragraph below --- train run 5 finished its $300$ seeds afterwards,
so the Results numbers there are the ones that are stale, not this bar. The winning weight is
$10^{3}$ under both candidate score rules (final reward $38.6412$; whole-run mean $17.1492$), so the
choice of rule does not move which configuration defines the bar.}

\paragraph{Pipeline.}
Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun5-pipeline} traces the original RND data flow --- environment step, next
state, observation whitening, frozen target and trainable predictor, per-dimension error, readout,
forward-filter reward normalization, $\beta$-scaling, and the sum with the extrinsic reward feeding
the RL update --- with CleanRL's departures marked as callouts: its predictor \emph{update} feeds
the stored current-step observation rather than the next observation; its convolutional LeakyReLU
slope is $0.01$; it clips gradients at global norm $0.5$; and it normalizes advantages per
minibatch. The \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} arm follows the original everywhere the figure's solid path runs (next-state
input throughout, no gradient clip), on SAC's single reward stream.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{code/2026-07-20_run5-writeup/rnd_pipeline.pdf}
\caption{The original RND pipeline (solid path): an environment step yields the next state $s'$,
whitened by a running mean/std and clipped; the frozen target $f(s')$ and the trainable predictor
$\hat f(s')$ give the per-dimension error $e$, read out as the bonus $b$, normalized by the running
std $\sigma_F$ of the forward-filtered intrinsic return, scaled by $\beta$, and added to the
extrinsic reward for the RL update. The predictor-gradient path (blue dashed) terminates only at the
predictor; the RMS statistic updates are dotted. CleanRL's departures from the original are marked as
dashed callouts. The \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} arm follows the solid path on SAC's single reward stream.}
\label{fig:trainrun5-pipeline}
\end{figure}

columns share a value; ``---'' marks a knob a source does not use. Architecture is written as one
network-pipeline string per net. There is no continuous-control column for the two Atari references:

(verified), so ExPLORe supplies the state-input reference. The \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} arm follows the RND paper on
every non-architecture knob (readout, Adam $10^{-4}$/$\epsilon\,10^{-8}$, warm-up, reward norm) and
the original \emph{repository code} on the update proportion ($1.0$); it takes the train-run-3 RND
widths but keeps the original's deeper-predictor asymmetry (one extra $128$-wide block). In
the reward-norm row, $\gamma_{\mathrm{int}}$ is the discount of the reward-normalization filter of

columns it appears only inside that filter --- the ``$-$'' in the
$\gamma_{\mathrm{int}}/\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}$ row below refers to the discount of a separate
intrinsic value head, which the single-head SAC setup does not have.

$15$-value grid, raced against the $10^{-4}$ winner's own number (see the paragraph below the
table).}}

that dict is train run 5's own \texttt{ORIGSMALL\_\allowbreak PARAMS} with one field changed, and
\texttt{slurm/\allowbreak test\_\allowbreak run\_\allowbreak queue\_\allowbreak convention.py}
asserts that \texttt{rnd\_\allowbreak lr} is the ONLY field that differs, so every ``same'' cell in
the new column is machine-checked rather than asserted by hand. The environment is the named setup
\texttt{initial\_\allowbreak single\_\allowbreak large\_\allowbreak pointmaze\_\allowbreak max\_\allowbreak 400},
which \texttt{slurm/\allowbreak test\_\allowbreak pointmaze\_\allowbreak env\_\allowbreak equivalence.py}
verifies reproduces train run 5's environment field for field, including the resolved goal cell
$(1,10)$ and start cell $(7,1)$. The struck ``$\beta$ swept'' cell is replaced by the eight values
themselves; ``swept'' alone did not say what was swept over. Caveat: results for the new column do
not exist yet --- the sweep was submitted 2026-08-05 and its bonus-weight table will be filled in
when the seeds land.}

\paragraph{Results \sout{(partial snapshot 2026-07-22; the 3000-run sweep is still draining its long
tail, so the per-arm seed counts $n$ below are interim, not the full 300 seeds --- the numbers
will firm up but are not expected to move much)}\claudeedit{(final for the $10^{-4}$ arms at their
full $300$ seeds; the addendum sweep STOPPED 2026-08-08 before its seed targets, so its rows are
final where they stand: $10^{-3}$ at about $40$ seeds, $10^{-2}$ at about $11$)}.}
Table~\ref{tab:trainrun5-results} reports the \sout{three}\claudeedit{five} run-5 arms --- the winning \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$}
$\beta$, the \runref{run-3.2.2 benchmark} (Adam, no reward normalization), and the \runref{run-3.2.3 reward-norm}
(Adam, reward normalization) --- on the final
training-episode extrinsic reward $\bar R$ (mean over seeds, $\mathrm{SE}=s/\sqrt{n}$), with the
old-run reference rows beneath.  Recall $\mathrm{succ.}$ is the fraction of a
configuration's seeds whose final training reward exceeds $5$ (defined for
run~3.2.1, Section~\ref{sec:train-run-3-2-1}).  Table~\ref{tab:trainrun5-betasweep} gives all eight
\runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} $\beta$ values \sout{with the beta-race prune verdict}\claudeedit{---
$\{10^{-2}, 10^{-1}, 0.5, 1, 10, 10^{2}, 10^{3}, 10^{4}\}$ --- with the verdict of the race between
them}, and
Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun5-curve} draws the reward-over-training curves.  Reading the snapshot:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Reward normalization leads, and reproduces its old result.}  The \runref{run-3.2.3 reward-norm} arm reaches
  \sout{$\bar R=47.56$ ($n=233$, $\mathrm{succ.}=0.89$)}\claudeedit{$\bar R=46.84$ ($n=300$, $\mathrm{succ.}=0.88$)}, the top of the table --- above the winning
  \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} arm and above the \runref{run-3.2.2 benchmark}, and slightly above its own old
  reference ($43.71$, run~3.2.4).
\item \textbf{The \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} arm lands between the two previous-RND arms.}  At its winning weight
  $\beta=10^3$ the faithful-original arm scores \sout{$\bar R=39.92$ ($n=226$,
  $\mathrm{succ.}=0.79$)}\claudeedit{$\bar R=38.64$ ($n=300$, $\mathrm{succ.}=0.78$)} --- above the \runref{run-3.2.2 benchmark} (\sout{$36.19$}\claudeedit{$35.16$}) but below the \runref{run-3.2.3 reward-norm}, so on this maze the reward
  normalization buys more than the remaining original-RND components (observation
  normalization, the deeper predictor, the mean-squared readout) recover.
\item \textbf{The bonus weight is strongly single-peaked.}  Across the eight $\beta$ the mean
  reward traces an inverted-U (Table~\ref{tab:trainrun5-betasweep}): every $\beta\le1$ sits
  below $8$, the curve climbs through $\beta=10$ ($11.84$) and $\beta=10^2$ (\sout{$29.14$}\claudeedit{$28.73$}) to a peak
  at $\beta=10^3$ (\sout{$39.92$}\claudeedit{$38.64$}), then falls back to $18.10$ at $\beta=10^4$.  The \sout{beta race}\claudeedit{race between the eight weights} pruned
  the seven off-peak weights and kept only $\beta=10^3$, which is running to the full seed
  budget.
\end{enumerate}

\input{../train_runs/2026-07-20-16-44_run_5_baseline_rnd-next-state__origsmall-mse-mean-lr1e-4-out128-predextra1-leaky0.2-warmupenv6400-rewardnorm-beta1e-2to1e4+0.5-prune30__C2-adam-mse-b100__N1-rewardnorm-b1e4__seed600-899_1Mstep_cpu-nolim/analysis/plots/beta_sweep_table.tex}
\caption{Train run~5, the eight \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} bonus weights
\claudeedit{$\beta\in\{10^{-2}, 10^{-1}, 0.5, 1, 10, 10^{2}, 10^{3}, 10^{4}\}$} on the final
training-episode extrinsic reward $\bar R$ (mean over seeds, $\mathrm{SE}=s/\sqrt{n}$), with the
\sout{beta-race}\claudeedit{race} verdict.  The race gives each $\beta$ at least $30$ seeds, then \sout{prunes}\claudeedit{stops} a $\beta$
once its one-sided $99\%$ upper confidence limit falls below the running-best mean; the sole
survivor $\beta=10^3$ runs to the full seed budget.  \sout{Partial snapshot --- pruned $\beta$ stop at
their pruned-time $n$.}\claudeedit{A stopped $\beta$ keeps the $n$ it had reached when it was
stopped, so the $n$ column is not constant down the table.}
\claudeedit{The two right-hand blocks are new: the same weight at Adam $10^{-3}$ and at Adam
$10^{-2}$, from the addendum run. All three grids share seven of the eight weights --- the addendum
grid runs $10^{-3}$ to $10^{4}$ at $1$ and $3$ per decade and has no $0.5$, so that row reads N/A
rather than a blank. Reading across, the $10^{-4}$ and $10^{-3}$ columns trace the same inverted-U
and peak at the same $\beta=10^{3}$, and no shared weight separates them: the largest single gap,
at $\beta=1$ ($6.50$ against $3.03$), is $1.3$ standard errors of the difference, and every other
weight is under $0.6$. The $10^{-3}$ column is interim at
$39$--$41$ seeds per weight, against $116$--$300$ on the left, so its standard errors are two to
three times larger and none of these gaps is established. The $10^{-2}$ column, final at $10$--$12$ seeds
per weight (the addendum stopped 2026-08-08), does something the other two do not: its inverted-U
PEAKS TWO DECADES LOWER, at
$\beta=10$ ($24.81$) rather than $\beta=10^{3}$, and it collapses where they are strongest ---
$6.60$ at $\beta=10^{3}$ and $0.36$ at $\beta=10^{4}$, against $38.64$ and $18.10$ at $10^{-4}$.
So on this task the predictor learning rate and the bonus weight are not independent knobs: raising
the rate by two decades moves the best weight down by two decades, and holding the weight fixed
while raising the rate destroys the arm. Its peak is also lower than either of theirs, though at
$11$ seeds and a standard error of $10.36$ that particular gap is about one standard error and is
not established.}}

% ---- Run 5: ground-truth oracles vs best RND (hand-built, snapshot 2026-07-22) ----
% Sources: run-2 data/local (97 gt seeds), run-3.2.2 folder oracle sweeps
% 2026-07-09-00-15 (1/sqrt(n)) and 2026-07-09-00-27 (1/n) at seeds 200-499, and the
% run-5 set-baseline sweep (reward-norm arm). Recompute when run 5 finishes its long tail.
\paragraph{Ground-truth oracles vs.\ the best RND arm.}
Table~\ref{tab:trainrun5-oracle-vs-rnd} places the run-5 winner (the \runref{run-3.2.3 reward-norm}
configuration) next to the ground-truth
count-based oracles (recall the count exponent $\alpha$ of Section~\ref{sec:count-based}:
the bonus is $\min(1,n^{\alpha})$, so $\alpha=-0.5$ gives $\min(1,1/\sqrt{n})$ and $\alpha=-1$
gives $\min(1,1/n)$), all trained on the same task and step budget
($10^{6}$ steps, \texttt{PointMaze\_\allowbreak Large-v3}, top-right goal). Define, per
configuration:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
\item the \emph{final reward} $\bar R$ --- the final extrinsic reward of each seed, averaged over
  seeds ($\mathrm{SE}=s/\sqrt{n}$). For the reward-norm row and the oracle re-runs this is the
  training-window mean over the last $100$ training episodes (the run-3-onward scoring metric);
  the $97$ run-2 seeds inside the merged row instead recorded a standalone $100$-episode
  evaluation --- the two protocols agree on the matched configuration (Welch's $t$-test,
  $p=0.48$);
\item the \emph{episode success rate} --- the share of a seed's last $100$ training episodes with
  extrinsic reward $>0$, averaged over seeds;
\item the \emph{seed success rate} --- the share of seeds whose final reward is $>0$ (the agent
  still reaches the goal at the end of training); this is a lower threshold than the
  $\mathrm{succ.}>5$ column of Table~\ref{tab:trainrun5-results};
\item the \emph{visit coverage} --- the number of distinct position cells visited during
  training, divided by the $46$ reachable cells of the $9\times12$ grid
  (Section~\ref{sec:env-count}), at the end of training.
\end{itemize}
The merged first row pools train run~2 (\S\ref{sec:train-run-2}, seeds $3$--$199$, $n=97$) with
the $\alpha=-0.5$ oracle re-run stored in the run-3.2.2 run folder (seeds $200$--$499$, $n=300$,
launched 2026-07-09): the two share the environment, the algorithm and bonus
(\texttt{gt\_\allowbreak position\_\allowbreak velocity}, $\alpha=-0.5$, $\beta=1$), the trainer,
and the step budget; they differ only in whether a standalone evaluation also ran; their seed
ranges are disjoint; and their reward and coverage distributions are statistically
indistinguishable (Welch's $t$-test, reward $p=0.48$, coverage $p=0.46$) --- so pooling is
justified. The $\alpha=-1$ oracle is a different bonus shape and stays separate. The reward-norm row is a
later 2026-07-22 snapshot than Table~\ref{tab:trainrun5-results} ($n=253$ vs.\ $233$ seeds
finished), so its numbers differ slightly. Reading the table: the reward-norm arm reaches about $71\%$ of the
merged oracle's reward, and covers slightly \emph{more} of the maze than either oracle.

\paragraph{Substitution of previous RND runs.}
Two run-5 arms (the \runref{run-3.2.2 benchmark} and the \runref{run-3.2.3 reward-norm}) re-run configurations
that earlier training runs already measured, so
each old run can be tested for poolability against its run-5 fresh line.  Define the test on the
final training-episode reward: for one old run versus the run-5 arm with the same
\texttt{config\_key}, report the Welch $t$-test $p$, Cohen's $d$, and the two-sample Kolmogorov--Smirnov
statistic.  The pre-registered rule (fixed before the sweep) is that an old run is
\emph{substantially different} --- and so is \emph{not} pooled --- iff $p<0.01$ \emph{and}
$\lvert d\rvert>0.3$; a run that passes neither gate (not substantially different) may be pooled
after an executed-path code-diff audit confirms the run code matches.
Table~\ref{tab:trainrun5-substitution} reports the four tests.  Three old runs (the benchmark's
run-3.2.2 data, the reward-norm's run-3.2.3 and run-3.2.4 data) are statistically
indistinguishable from their run-5 lines
($p\ge0.3$, $\lvert d\rvert\le0.12$) and are candidates for pooling; the benchmark's older
run-3.1.1 data
differs sharply ($p\approx5\times10^{-10}$, $d=0.99$) and is kept separate.  The actual pooling
is deferred to sweep completion, when the fresh lines reach their full seed counts and the
code-diff audit is run; until then the reference rows in Table~\ref{tab:trainrun5-results} are
shown separately, not pooled.  Every old run folder stays byte-untouched on disk regardless of
the verdict.

\subsection{Train run 6 (the four train-run-1.2 algorithms on the run-5 PointMaze task)}
\label{sec:train-run-6}

\claudeedit{Train run~1.2 built four algorithm arms and raced them on two AntMaze environments,
where nothing converges (Section~\ref{sec:pm-am-nonconvergence}). Train run~6 asks the same
comparison question on the task where the original RND demonstrably works: train run~5's own
PointMaze Large top-right task, unchanged (episode cap $400$, discount $0.999$, raw sparse
reward, the \texttt{initial\_\allowbreak single\_\allowbreak large\_\allowbreak
pointmaze\_\allowbreak max\_\allowbreak 400} registry entry). The question: does any of the four
variations beat the \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} on its own ground, under its own
score --- the final training-episode reward, i.e.\ the last evaluation's mean extrinsic return
over the previous $100$ training episodes? The original RND is NOT re-run: its $300$-seed final
result ($\bar R = 38.6412$ at bonus weight $10^{3}$) is the frozen reference this run races
against. What IS swept: the four train-run-1.2 arms exactly as that run defined them (their
parameter dictionaries are copied verbatim from that run's queue builder and pinned by a unit
test), each over the two plain-SGD predictor learning rates $\{10^{-3}, 10^{-2}\}$ and the
fifteen bonus weights $\beta \in \{$$10^{-3}$, $3{\times}10^{-3}$, $10^{-2}$,
$3{\times}10^{-2}$, $10^{-1}$, $3{\times}10^{-1}$, $1$, $3$, $10$, $30$, $10^{2}$,
$3{\times}10^{2}$, $10^{3}$, $3{\times}10^{3}$, $10^{4}$$\}$ --- $4 \times 2 \times 15 = 120$
configurations, on fresh seeds $1200$--$1499$ (disjoint from run~5's $600$--$899$ and the
learning-rate addendum's $900$--$1199$), $10^{6}$ steps per run.}

\claudeedit{The race is two-phase. Define $\bar R_c$, $s_c$ and $n_c$ as configuration $c$'s mean
final reward, standard deviation and completed-seed count, and $\bar R^{\star} = 38.6412$ the
frozen bar. Phase 1: from $n_c \ge 20$ completed seeds, $c$ is truncated at the first controller
tick at which
\begin{align}
\bar R_c + 2.576\,\frac{s_c}{\sqrt{n_c}} \;<\; \bar R^{\star},
\end{align}
that is, when its one-sided $99\%$ upper confidence limit falls below what the original RND
already achieved; survivors race to $100$ seeds. Phase 2: once every configuration of an arm is
resolved, the arm's best-mean surviving configuration CONTINUES to $300$ seeds; every other
survivor of that arm stops at $100$. So the sweep spends its budget in proportion to promise:
weak configurations cost $20$ seeds, competitive ones $100$, and exactly one configuration per
arm gets the full $300$-seed measurement that matches the reference's precision.
Table~\ref{tab:trainrun6-algos} gives the full configuration; Table~\ref{tab:trainrun6-results}
and Figure~\ref{fig:trainrun6-curve} report the results from the live records and are regenerated
as the sweep advances.}

\clearpage
\section{Convergence rate runs}
\label{sec:convergence-rate-runs}

The training runs of Section~\ref{sec:train-runs} score an intrinsic bonus by the extrinsic
reward it earns inside the full RL loop.  This section asks a more basic question about the
bonus itself: \emph{how fast does the intrinsic bonus decay when the predictor is trained
repeatedly on the same inputs?}  The count-based reading of RND treats the bonus at a state as
a stand-in for one over the square root of that state's visit count, so the decay \emph{rate}
--- not just the final level --- is the property the RL runs implicitly rely on.  The runs in
this section therefore strip away the RL loop entirely: a fixed set of maze points is distilled
for a fixed number of full-batch optimizer steps, and the per-point bonus curve is measured and
fitted.  The fitting methodology comes first because every run in this section reports its
results through it.
\clearpage

\subsection{Rate-fitting methodology}
\label{sec:convergence-fit-method}

Throughout this section, define $n$ as the optimizer-step index (one full-batch update per
step) and $y(n)$ as a normalized bonus curve evaluated at the stored checkpoint steps, with
$y(0)=1$ by construction (each run subsection defines how its curves are built and normalized).
The curves are typically \emph{decreasing then stabilizing}, so a fit must separate the
decreasing part (the quantity of interest) from the stabilizing part.  Define $c \ge 0$ as the
floor (the level the curve stabilizes at), $a > 0$ as the amplitude, $n_0 \ge 0$ as an onset
shift, and $\alpha > 0$ as the decay exponent.  The fitted model is the power decay with a
floor,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:convergence-power-model}
y(n) \;=\; c + a\,(n + n_0)^{-\alpha},
\end{align}
and the reported \emph{slope} of a curve is $-\alpha$ (the log--log slope of the decaying term).
Only this model is fitted.  The procedure:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Fit the raw curves with the floor; never a straight line on $\log y$.}  A
  straight-line regression on $\log y$ has no way to represent $c$: near the plateau the
  log-curve bends flat and drags the estimated slope toward $0$, so the line fit mixes the
  decreasing part with the stabilizing part.  Fitting
  Eq.~\eqref{eq:convergence-power-model} instead absorbs the plateau into $c$, and $\alpha$
  measures only the decreasing part.  Implementation: nonlinear least squares of the full model
  against the checkpoint values, with residuals taken on $\log y$ so every decade of the curve
  counts equally (still a nonlinear floored fit, not a line fit), bounded parameters, and
  multiple starts.
\item \textbf{Fixed burn-in $n \ge 2$.}  The fit window is steps $2$ through $4096$ for every
  curve --- one fixed number, stated here before any results and never tuned per curve or per
  configuration.  Only step $1$ is excluded (step $0$ is the normalization baseline), so a
  curve has $78$ in-window checkpoints on the grid of
  Section~\ref{sec:convergence-run-1}.  Early deviations from a pure power law are handled by
  the model itself ($n_0$ and $c$) and made visible by the local exponent below, not by
  trimming the window.
\item \textbf{Two fitting granularities, reported separately.}  Define $S$ as the number of
  seeds of a configuration.  (i)~\emph{Per-seed fits}: for each seed, average the normalized
  per-point curves over the point set to get one curve per seed, and fit it --- $S$ independent
  slopes, reported as their mean.  (ii)~\emph{Seed-average fit}: average the normalized curves
  over all seeds and points to get the single mean curve the figures plot, and fit it once.
  Both are shown because averaging heterogeneous decays can produce a mean curve whose exponent
  is none of the individual ones; agreement between the two reports is evidence the estimate is
  stable, and a gap flags heterogeneity across seeds.
\item \textbf{Uncertainty $=$ standard error over the seeds.}  Every reported slope and every
  plotted curve carries the standard error over the $S$ per-seed values: the per-seed slopes
  (or, for a curve, the per-seed means at each checkpoint) give a mean and
  $\mathrm{SE} = s/\sqrt{S}$, with $s$ their standard deviation.  The seed is the independent
  unit --- all points within a seed share one network draw --- so no finer axis would give an
  honest error bar.
\item \textbf{Local exponent plot.}  Recall $c$ is the fitted floor.  Define the local exponent
  between successive checkpoints as
  \begin{align}
  \alpha_{\mathrm{eff}}(n) \;=\; -\,\frac{\Delta \log\,\bigl(y(n) - c\bigr)}{\Delta \log n},
  \end{align}
  computed on the seed-average curve with that fit's $c$ (masked where $y \le c$).  A single
  global $\alpha$ can hide an early geometric phase followed by a floor or a power-law tail;
  the local exponent makes the phases visible --- a true power law is a horizontal line at
  $\alpha$, a geometric phase is a rising ramp, and the floor sends the raw-curve version
  toward $0$.
\end{enumerate}
\clearpage

\subsection{Convergence run 1: full-batch distillation on fixed point sets}
\label{sec:convergence-run-1}

Convergence run~1 measures the decay of the RND bonus under pure distillation --- no SAC, no
environment stepping, no replay buffer, no $\beta$: fixed points in, one full-batch gradient
step per update, $4096$ updates.

\paragraph{Environment and point sets.}
The maze is \texttt{PointMaze\_\allowbreak Large-v3} exactly as in every training run: $9$
cells high and $12$ cells wide (confirmed against the live environment), cell size $1\times1$,
world extent $x \in [-6, 6]$, $y \in [-4.5, 4.5]$, and cell $(\mathrm{row}, \mathrm{col})$
centered at $(\mathrm{col} - 5.5,\; 4 - \mathrm{row})$ with row~$0$ at the top and $y$ up.
The untrained-bonus figures of Section~\ref{sec:train-run-3-2-2}
(Figures~\ref{fig:trainrun3-2-2-initfield-vel} and~\ref{fig:trainrun3-2-2-bias-ablation})
discretized the extent into a $10\times10$ grid of $1.2\times0.9$ boxes, which does \emph{not}
align with the maze's own cells; this run uses maze-aligned grids instead, so the
visualization matches the grid (Figure~\ref{fig:convergence-run1-point-sets}).  Three point
sets, each trained as its own runs:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{env 1.1 (center square).}  The $1\times1$ world square centered at $(0,0)$,
  discretized into $10\times10$ sub-cell midpoints: $x, y \in \{-0.45, -0.35, \ldots, +0.45\}$
  (spacing $0.1$, the same midpoint convention as the earlier figures), $100$ points.  No maze
  cell is centered at $(0,0)$: the square's left half lies over wall cell $(4, 5)$ and only its
  right half in free cell $(4, 6)$.  It is kept regardless (a deliberate choice): the RND
  networks are ordinary functions of the observation, defined at wall points too, and $(0,0)$
  is the exact bottom of the untrained-bonus bowl, giving maximal contrast with the corner
  grid.
\item \textbf{env 1.2 (top-right cell).}  The top-right free cell $(1, 10)$ --- the fixed
  \texttt{top\_\allowbreak right} goal cell, world center $(4.5, 3.0)$ --- discretized the same
  way: $x \in \{4.05, \ldots, 4.95\}$, $y \in \{2.55, \ldots, 3.45\}$, $100$ points.
\item \textbf{env 2 (cell midpoints).}  The midpoint of every maze cell of the $9\times12$
  grid: $108$ points, wall cells included ($46$ free $+$ $62$ wall; storage is per point, so a
  free-cells-only view remains available at analysis time).
\end{enumerate}
Every point has $v_x = v_y = 0$, and the input to both networks is the raw $4$-vector
$[x, y, 0, 0]$: observation normalization is \emph{off} (unlike the training runs), so the
inputs are stationary across all $4096$ steps and the run has no environment coupling at all.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{code/2026-07-19-03-21_convergence-run1-point-sets/point_sets.pdf}
\caption{The three point sets of convergence run~1 on the maze's own $9\times12$ cell grid
(walls grey).  Black dots: env~2, the $108$ cell midpoints (wall cells included).  Blue: env~1.1,
$100$ sub-cell midpoints of the $1\times1$ square centered at $(0,0)$ --- its left half lies over
a wall cell, kept deliberately.  Orange: env~1.2, $100$ sub-cell midpoints of the top-right free
cell $(1, 10)$, the fixed goal cell.  Every point has $v_x = v_y = 0$.}
\label{fig:convergence-run1-point-sets}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Distillation training.}
Recall the predictor $g_\phi$ and the frozen target $f_\theta$ (both $4 \to 256 \to
\mathrm{ReLU} \to 128$, the architecture of every 3.2.x run), and recall the training objective
$L^{\mathrm{mse}}$ of Section~\ref{sec:methods}: the batch mean of $d_{\mathrm{mse}}(x;\phi) =
\frac12 \lVert g_\phi(x) - f_\theta(x) \rVert_2^2$ (no input whitening here, so $\tilde{x} =
x$).  One training step forwards \emph{all} $P$ points of the point set in a single batch and
takes one optimizer step on $L^{\mathrm{mse}}$; this is repeated for $4096$ steps through the
exact \texttt{RND.update} code path of the training runs (trainer:
\texttt{convergence\_\allowbreak train.py}).  The training objective is always the squared
quantity --- the $\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{l2}}$ readout below is logging only and never enters
training.  Given the configuration and the seed the entire run is deterministic (full batch, no
sampling, no dropout, observation normalization off): \emph{the initialization realization is
the only randomness}, and each of the $30$ seeds re-draws both networks.

\paragraph{Initialization schemes.}
Define $d$ as a layer's fan-in (its input width: $d = 4$ for the first Linear layer, $d = 256$
for the second).  Four schemes, applied to both networks:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{zero-bias --- RND paper $=$ CleanRL (project default).}  Orthogonal weights with gain
  $\sqrt{2}$ on every layer, all biases $0$.  Section~\ref{sec:train-run-3-2-2}
  (Table~\ref{tab:trainrun3-2-2-rnd-init}) verified that the original RND release, CleanRL, and
  this codebase share exactly this law.  Flag: \texttt{rnd\_\allowbreak bias\_\allowbreak
  init}$=$\texttt{zero}.
\item \textbf{pytorch-bias --- PyTorch-default biases (runs 3.2.3/3.2.4 scheme).}  Weights stay
  orthogonal with gain $\sqrt{2}$; every bias is drawn i.i.d.\ uniform on
  $[-1/\sqrt{d},\, +1/\sqrt{d}]$ (PyTorch's default bias law).  Flag:
  \texttt{rnd\_\allowbreak bias\_\allowbreak init}$=$\texttt{pytorch\_\allowbreak default}.
\item \textbf{pytorch-full --- full PyTorch default (new switch).}  Weights \emph{and} biases are drawn
  i.i.d.\ uniform on $[-1/\sqrt{d},\, +1/\sqrt{d}]$ --- for the weights this is what
  \texttt{nn.Linear}'s default (\texttt{kaiming\_\allowbreak uniform\_} with $a{=}\sqrt{5}$)
  reduces to, a law the document had not previously stated.  Note the repo's
  \texttt{pytorch\_\allowbreak default} bias flag alone (pytorch-bias) is \emph{not} the full PyTorch
  default; the new weight switch \texttt{rnd\_\allowbreak weight\_\allowbreak
  init}$=$\texttt{pytorch\_\allowbreak default} (default \texttt{orthogonal}, bit-identical to
  all prior runs) completes it, drawing from its own keyed stream so no existing draw shifts.
\item \textbf{normal-0.5 --- best of run 3.2.4.}  Weights orthogonal with gain $\sqrt{2}$; every bias
  $b = 0.5\,z$ with $z \sim N(0,1)$, i.e.\ $b \sim N(0,\, 0.25)$ --- the
  \texttt{normal\_\allowbreak 0.5} scheme of \runref{run-3.2.4 rank 1}, the fresh-seed winner of run
  3.2.4 (Section~\ref{sec:train-run-3-2-4}; preliminary at $n \approx 75$ of $100$ seeds, but
  the ranking has been stable across snapshots).
\end{enumerate}
The keyed bias draws of pytorch-bias and normal-0.5 are byte-identical to runs 3.2.3/3.2.4 at the same seed
(Section~\ref{sec:train-run-3-2-3}).

\paragraph{Optimizers.}
Recall the \runref{run-3.2.1 SGD-\(1/t\)} method (defined in
Section~\ref{sec:train-run-3-rnd-optimizers}, swept in run 3.2.1): define $t$ as the $0$-based
optimizer-step counter, $\eta_0$ as the initial learning rate (the exact step size at $t=0$),
$t_0$ as the schedule offset, and $\eta_{\min}$ as the learning-rate floor; SGD-$1/t$ is plain
SGD with $\eta_t = \max(\eta_{\min},\, \eta_0/(1 + t/t_0))$ and $\eta_{\min} = 0$.  The rate is
nearly constant before $t \approx t_0$, halves at $t = t_0$, and follows the $\eta_0 t_0/t$
tail afterward (running-average, count-style behavior).  Within a $4096$-step run the final
rate factors are $0.024$ for $t_0 = 10^2$ (mostly tail), $0.196$ for $t_0 = 10^3$, and $0.71$
for $t_0 = 10^4$ (near-constant) --- so the $t_0$ grid deliberately includes $10^2$ on top of
run 3.2.3's $\{10^3, 10^4\}$.  The $\eta_0$ grid brackets run 3.2.4's best value $0.1$ from
below.  Adam runs at fixed PyTorch defaults with its learning rate swept over the default
$10^{-3}$ and the two lower decades.  Table~\ref{tab:convergence-run1-optimizers} fixes both
arms.
