\paragraph{Logging.}
Define $j$ as a checkpoint index.  Each run stores the per-point readout only at the
checkpoint steps $\{0\} \cup \{\operatorname{round}(2^{j/8}) : j = 0, 1, \ldots, 96\}$
deduplicated --- quarter splits of a half-exponent log grid, $79$ distinct nonzero steps plus
the step-$0$ baseline ($80$ stored steps; at the small end neighboring exponents round to the
same integer and collapse).  ``Step $n$'' means after $n$ optimizer updates.  Each run writes
one JSON record (atomic replace at every checkpoint with \texttt{completed}$=$\texttt{False},
final flush \texttt{True} --- the project's checkpointed-logging convention), holding the
configuration, the point coordinates, and one row of $P$ raw per-point values per stored step.
Two details: the logged value is the \emph{exact} residual norm --- the readout's $10^{-8}$
numerical clamp (which would floor deeply converged values at $10^{-4}$) is dropped in logging
only, training untouched; and a run whose loss or readout turns non-finite stops early with a
\texttt{diverged} flag (kept out of every fit and reported as an exclusion count).

\paragraph{Normalization and the reference slope.}
Define $b_i(n)$ as the logged readout at point $x_i$ after $n$ updates,
$b_i(n) = \mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{l2}}(x_i;\phi_n) = \lVert g_{\phi_n}(x_i) - f_\theta(x_i)
\rVert_2$.  Every point --- whichever point set it belongs to --- starts from a slightly
different untrained bonus $b_i(0)$ (the untrained field is not flat), so the normalization is
\emph{point-wise}: define $y_i(n) = b_i(n) / b_i(0)$, giving every point a line that starts at
exactly $1$.  Define $S = 30$ as the seed count and $P$ as the point count; the plotted curve
of a configuration is the mean over seeds and points,
\begin{align}
\bar{y}(n) \;=\; \frac{1}{S\,P} \sum_{s=1}^{S} \sum_{i=1}^{P} \frac{b_{s,i}(n)}{b_{s,i}(0)},
\end{align}
drawn on log--log axes (log of the l2 intrinsic value against log of the optimizer step) with
a standard-error band over the $30$ seeds.  Every such plot carries a \emph{grey solid}
reference line of slope $-1/2$: if the squared error at a point decays as $c'/n$ for some
constant $c'$, then $b(n) = \sqrt{2\,d_{\mathrm{mse}}} \propto n^{-1/2}$, so $\log b(n) =
-\tfrac12 \log n + \mathrm{const}$ --- a straight line of slope $-1/2$.  This is the
count-based analogy: a bonus proportional to one over the square root of the visit count, when
each full-batch step visits every point exactly once.  It is a reference, not a prediction:
full-batch gradient descent here is deterministic (no gradient noise), so the classical
stochastic-approximation rates need not apply --- measuring the actual decay is the point of
the run.

\paragraph{Results.}
The sweep completed 2026-07-19: all $4320$ runs finished (13 minutes wall on 192 workers, none
failed).  $180$ runs diverged and are excluded from every fit --- exactly the $6$ cells
$\times$ $30$ seeds of SGD-$1/t$ at $\eta_0 = 10^{-1}$ on env~2 for the two largest-scale
initializations (pytorch-full and normal-0.5), at every $t_0$: env~2's raw inputs reach $\lvert x\rvert = 5.5$
(no observation normalization), and the $0.1$-rate full-batch step is unstable there, while zero-bias
and pytorch-bias survive it.  The tables mark these cells.  Recall the fitted model of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:convergence-power-model}; every cell below reports the fitted slope
$-\hat\alpha$, and rows are ranked by closeness of the per-seed aggregate slope to the
reference $-1/2$.

env column of Table~\ref{tab:convergence-run1-opt-effect}, slopes averaged over the four
initializations within each seed (bars $=$ standard error over the 30 seeds; horizontal line at
$-1/2$).  Missing $\eta_0 = 10^{-1}$ points on the env~2 and aggregate panels are the diverged
cells.}
\label{fig:convergence-run1-opt-effect}

Findings:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{A near-perfect $-1/2$ exists.}  The top configuration --- zero-bias with
  SGD-$1/t$ at $\eta_0 = 10^{-2}$, $t_0 = 10^{2}$ --- fits an aggregate slope of
  $-0.503 \pm 0.005$ (seed-average fit $-0.502$): under the right decaying schedule the l2
  bonus tracks the one-over-square-root-of-visits reference almost exactly over three decades
  of steps.  Several SGD-$1/t$ cells cluster within $\pm 0.05$ of $-1/2$.
\item \textbf{Adam decays faster than $-1/2$ everywhere.}  The best Adam configuration (zero-bias,
  lr $10^{-3}$) fits $-0.83$ on the aggregate, and no Adam cell comes close to $-1/2$: under a
  constant adaptive rate the bonus falls off substantially faster than a count-based bonus
  would --- consistent with deterministic full-batch descent having no gradient noise to slow
  it.
\item \textbf{The two fit granularities agree where the mixture is homogeneous --- and flag
  where it is not.}  On the aggregate column the per-seed mean and the seed-average slope
  differ by a median of $0.002$ (maximum $0.04$) across the surviving configurations
  (Tables~\ref{tab:convergence-run1-slopes-perseed}
  and~\ref{tab:convergence-run1-slopes-seedavg}).  On env~1.2, however, the gap reaches
  $0.53$ for the $\eta_0 = 10^{-2}$, $t_0 = 10^{2}$ family: the top-right cell's per-seed
  curves are heterogeneous enough that the mean curve's exponent is none of the individual
  ones --- exactly the failure mode that reporting both granularities is designed to expose.
\item \textbf{The curves are not single power laws.}  The local-exponent panels show an early
  geometric ramp, a mid-range plateau, and a late steepening whose onset tracks $t_0$; the
  very steep small-$\eta_0$ slopes (down to $\approx -1.7$) reflect that late near-constant-rate
  geometric phase rather than any count-like decay.  The floor $c$ absorbs the stabilizing
  tail (e.g.\ $c \approx 0.026$ for normal-0.5 with $\eta_0 = 10^{-1}$, $t_0 = 10^{4}$ on env~1.2).
\item \textbf{The stability boundary is initialization-dependent.}  SGD-$1/t$ at
  $\eta_0 = 10^{-1}$ diverges on env~2 for pytorch-full and normal-0.5 (all seeds, every $t_0$) while zero-bias and pytorch-bias
  survive --- the large-coordinate raw inputs amplify the largest-scale initializations past
  the stable-step threshold.
\end{enumerate}

\clearpage
\section{point maze + ant maze}
\label{sec:pm-am}

\subsection{Train run 1}
\label{sec:pm-am-trainrun1-group}
\setcounter{subsubsection}{-1}

Train run~1 is the first family of runs on the point maze and ant maze environments, and this
subsection holds all of it: the environment specification the whole family shares
(subsubsection~\ref{sec:pm-am-envspec}), the sweep that has already been executed --- train
run~1.1 (subsubsection~\ref{sec:pm-am-trainrun1}) --- and the follow-up sweep it motivated ---
train run~1.2 (subsubsection~\ref{sec:pm-am-trainrun12}).

\subsubsection{Environment specification}
\label{sec:pm-am-envspec}

PointMaze and AntMaze both come from the Gymnasium-Robotics maze family
\citep{delazcano2023gymrobotics}, refactored from D4RL \citep{fu2020d4rl}: a maze built from
square cells in which the agent must reach a goal position and receives a sparse reward on arrival.
PointMaze moves a force-actuated two-dimensional ball; AntMaze replaces the ball with the quadruped
``Ant'' robot, so reaching the goal additionally requires learning legged locomotion. This project
uses four PointMaze maze sizes (\texttt{PointMaze\_\allowbreak UMaze-v3},
\texttt{PointMaze\_\allowbreak Open-v3}, \texttt{PointMaze\_\allowbreak Medium-v3},
\texttt{PointMaze\_\allowbreak Large-v3}) and the four matching AntMaze sizes
(\texttt{AntMaze\_\allowbreak UMaze-v5} through \texttt{AntMaze\_\allowbreak Large-v5}) --- eight
base environments. Each base environment is instantiated with two fixed start/goal sets (one per
pair of opposite corners), so there are $8 \times 2 = 16$ named environment configurations, each
named by its start corner (Table~\ref{tab:pm-am-envspec}). The choice to run RND on maze navigation
follows ExPLORe \citep{li2023accelerating}, which ran state-input RND on the D4RL AntMaze tasks;
every reward curve in this section is this project's own run, and nothing is reused from ExPLORe.

\paragraph{Goal dynamics (code-verified).} The maze environment keeps exactly one active goal at a
time, sampled at reset. A ``diverse'' variant samples that one goal from several candidate cells but
still exposes a single two-dimensional goal, so the agent is never asked to reach more than one goal
at once. Two constructor flags govern what happens when the agent reaches the goal. With the library
defaults (\texttt{continuing\_task=True}, \texttt{reset\_target=False}) the episode never terminates
on success and the goal never moves: the agent keeps collecting the per-step reward for every step
it stays inside the success radius, until the time limit truncates the episode --- this is exactly
the project's previous PointMaze configuration, whose mean extrinsic reward therefore counts steps
spent at the goal. The environment configurations in this section instead set
\texttt{continuing\_task=False}, so the episode terminates the moment the agent is within $0.45$~m of
the goal.\footnote{The installed docstring and the online documentation describe
\texttt{reset\_target} the opposite way from the code: they state that \texttt{True} keeps the goal
in place, whereas the code regenerates the goal after success when \texttt{True} and leaves it fixed
when \texttt{False} (\texttt{point\_maze.py}, \texttt{maze\_v4.py}). The code is authoritative; this
section states the code behavior.} The only place a goal moves mid-episode is the
\texttt{update\_goal} branch, which fires only when \texttt{continuing\_task} and
\texttt{reset\_target} are both true and there is more than one candidate goal cell; under this
section's settings that branch is unreachable, so the goal is fixed at one exact cell center for the
whole run.

\paragraph{Metrics.} Every train run in this section logs the following five metrics for each
environment configuration it uses. Define $r_t$ as the (shifted) extrinsic reward at env step $t$,
$R = \sum_t r_t$ as the episode return, $k$ as the number of env steps the episode takes with the
goal-reaching step included, and $T_{\max}$ as the episode time limit (the registered default of
Table~\ref{tab:pm-am-envspec}).
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Episode return} $R = \sum_t r_t$. A successful episode earns $-1$ on each of its first
  $k-1$ steps and $0$ on the terminating step, so $R = -(k-1)$; a failed episode earns $-1$ on all
  $T_{\max}$ steps, so $R = -T_{\max}$.
\item \textbf{Success rate} $= N_{\mathrm{succ}} / N$, where $N_{\mathrm{succ}}$ is the number of
  episodes that end with \texttt{terminated=True} (the agent reached the goal) and $N$ is the total
  number of episodes.
\item \textbf{Steps to reach the goal} $= k$ (the terminating step included), averaged over
  successful episodes only; on a success $R = -(k-1)$, so steps-to-goal $= 1 - R$.
\item \textbf{Maze-cell coverage} $= C_{\mathrm{cell}} / C_{\mathrm{cell}}^{\mathrm{open}}$, where
  $C_{\mathrm{cell}}$ is the number of distinct open maze cells the agent has visited and
  $C_{\mathrm{cell}}^{\mathrm{open}}$ is the total number of open maze cells; one cell is $1$~m for
  PointMaze and $4$~m for AntMaze.
\item \textbf{$1$~m $\times$ $1$~m coverage} $= C_{1\mathrm{m}} / C_{1\mathrm{m}}^{\mathrm{open}}$,
  where $C_{1\mathrm{m}}$ is the number of distinct open $1$~m squares visited and
  $C_{1\mathrm{m}}^{\mathrm{open}}$ is the total number of open $1$~m squares. This equals metric~4
  for PointMaze (its cells are already $1$~m); for AntMaze each open $4$~m cell contains exactly
  $16$ such squares.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{D4RL vs.\ the current implementation.} This project builds its maze environments from the
current Gymnasium-Robotics library \citep{delazcano2023gymrobotics}; ExPLORe
\citep{li2023accelerating} built its AntMaze tasks from the older D4RL \citep{fu2020d4rl}
implementation. The two share the maze family but differ in several implementation details.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Success radius.} D4RL declares success within $0.5$~m of the goal; the current library
  uses a fixed $0.45$~m radius, independent of the cell size.
\item \textbf{Goal placement noise.} D4RL redraws the goal position each reset with one-sided
  uniform noise up to $1.5$~m per axis (to reduce evaluation variance); the current configurations
  set the per-reset position noise to $0$, so the goal sits at its exact cell center every episode.
\item \textbf{Start placement noise.} D4RL places the ant at the start cell with $\pm0.1$ position
  noise and small velocity noise; the current configurations use exact-center starts (noise $0$).
\item \textbf{Termination and time-limit API.} D4RL uses the old single-flag gym step API: at the
  time limit it returns \texttt{done=True} with \texttt{info["TimeLimit.truncated"]=True}, and
  ExPLORe maps that to a bootstrapped value. The current library uses the Gymnasium five-tuple API,
  returning \texttt{terminated=True} on goal reach and \texttt{truncated=True} at the time limit, and
  SB3's replay buffer bootstraps the value at truncations (\texttt{handle\_\allowbreak
  timeout\_\allowbreak termination=True}).
\item \textbf{Reward shift and hidden goal (shared).} Both subtract $1$ from the sparse reward
  ($-1$ per step, $0$ on the success step) and both keep the goal out of the agent's state; the
  current configurations match ExPLORe on these two counts.
\item \textbf{Simulator and bindings.} D4RL runs on the older \texttt{mujoco-py} bindings; the
  current library runs on MuJoCo $3.1.6$ through Gymnasium-Robotics $1.3.1$.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Versions.} The project uses the newest released version of each environment.
\texttt{PointMaze\_\allowbreak *-v3} is the newest PointMaze version (nothing newer exists up to
library release $1.4.2$), and the installed Gymnasium-Robotics $1.3.1$ provides the newest AntMaze
version \texttt{AntMaze\_\allowbreak *-v5} (it registers v3/v4/v5). Library releases
$1.4.0$--$1.4.2$ changed nothing for these two environments --- their notes cover dependency floors,
Python-version support, legacy MuJoCo v2/v3 migration, and hot fixes --- so no upgrade is required.

\subsubsection{Train run 1.1}
\label{sec:pm-am-trainrun1}

The point maze + ant maze train run~1.1 tests the following hypothesis.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{The ground-truth bonus performs better than RND on every PointMaze configuration.} On
  all four PointMaze configurations in this run (the \texttt{start\_\allowbreak bottom\_\allowbreak
  left} set), SAC $+$ \texttt{gt\_\allowbreak position\_\allowbreak velocity} (the oracle visit-count
  bonus) reaches better performance than SAC $+$ RND, compared at each algorithm's best bonus weight
  on the run's primary metrics (episode return and success rate).
\end{enumerate}

Train run~1.1 uses only the set-(1) \texttt{start\_\allowbreak bottom\_\allowbreak left}
configurations --- $8$ of the $16$. Table~\ref{tab:pm-am-trainrun1-envs} is a frozen copy of
Table~\ref{tab:pm-am-envspec} with the set-(2) rows removed:
subsubsection~\ref{sec:pm-am-envspec} may be extended later, but this copy stays fixed as the
record of what train run~1.1 used.

\end{table}

\paragraph{Results (sweep ended at the 2026-07-31 snapshot).}
\label{sec:pm-am-trainrun1-interim}

The sweep was ended at this snapshot, on 2026-07-31, eight days after launch; the numbers below
cover the runs completed by then and are the run's final data. Only records a run wrote at its natural end
(\texttt{completed=true}) enter any aggregate: when a worker is killed mid-run (for example by a
Slurm job reaching its time limit), the run's partial checkpoint record is skipped and the run is
re-queued and re-run in full, so no infrastructure-killed run enters any denominator, and no run
has failed permanently ($0$ in the queue's failed state). The owner's and the collaborator's
workers share one queue, so the numbers pool every submitter's completed runs. As of this
snapshot, $13780$ runs are complete. Of the $308$ configurations, $202$ had been pruned and $106$
were still racing when the sweep ended; no (environment, algorithm) cell reached the winner phase
(which would have started only when every surviving configuration of the cell had $\ge 100$
completed seeds). So the racing stopped at the seed counts shown in the tables: a cell's best
configuration is the best at these counts, least settled in the cells that were still racing many
configurations (named in finding 4 below).
Table~\ref{tab:pm-am-run1-interim-status} gives the racing state per (environment, algorithm)
cell at the stop.

\paragraph{How to read the metrics tables.}
Tables~\ref{tab:pm-am-run1-interim-pointmaze} and \ref{tab:pm-am-run1-interim-antmaze} show, for
each environment, one row per algorithm at its \emph{best} configuration --- best $=$
highest mean whole-run reward among the algorithm's configurations, computed over completed runs
--- with the swept knob (the bonus weight $\beta$) spelled out in the row label. Define, for one
run (one seed of one configuration), $R_e$ as the total extrinsic reward of its $e$-th completed
training episode and $E$ as its number of completed training episodes. The metric columns are:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{whole-run reward} $\bar{R} = \frac{1}{E}\sum_{e=1}^{E} R_e$ --- the ranking column
  of the tables and the racing prune score;
\item \textbf{last-100 reward} $\bar{R}_{100} = \frac{1}{100}\sum_{e=E-99}^{E} R_e$ (over all
  episodes when $E < 100$) --- the end-of-training performance;
\item \textbf{success rate} --- the fraction of the run's last $100$ episodes that reach the
  goal;
\item \textbf{steps to goal} --- the mean number of steps to reach the goal over the run's
  successful episodes (whole run); a run with zero successful episodes is excluded from this
  column, and when that leaves fewer runs than the row's $N$, the included count is shown as
  ($n$=..);
\item \textbf{maze-cell coverage \%} --- the percentage of open maze cells visited at least once
  by the end of the run (the visit counts are cumulative, so this is the coverage after the final
  episodes);
\item \textbf{$1$\,m coverage \%} (AntMaze table only) --- the same percentage over $1$-meter
  squares; the PointMaze maze cell is $1$~m, so on PointMaze this column would repeat the
  maze-cell column and is dropped.
\end{enumerate}
Define $n$ as the number of completed runs of the row's configuration and $s$ as the sample
standard deviation of a metric over those $n$ runs. Each table cell shows the metric's mean $\pm$
its standard error $s/\sqrt{n}$ over those runs, and the final column $N$ is that $n$. Rows are
ranked by whole-run reward, best first (the single sort arrow marks the ranking column). Per
metric column, within each environment block, the best value is \textbf{bold} and the second best
\underline{underlined}; higher is better in every column except steps to goal (lower is better);
$N$ is bookkeeping and is never marked.

\paragraph{Findings.}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{The hypothesis is currently supported on two of the four PointMaze
  configurations.} On whole-run reward (the ranking and prune score),
  \texttt{gt\_\allowbreak position\_\allowbreak velocity} leads SAC $+$ RND's best configuration
  on UMaze ($-98.09 \pm 0.08$ vs $-104.21 \pm 0.35$) and Medium ($-129.38 \pm 0.25$ vs
  $-129.68 \pm 0.31$, a gap smaller than one combined standard error), while SAC $+$ RND
  currently leads on Open ($-80.14 \pm 0.07$ vs $-80.24 \pm 0.06$, also about one combined
  standard error) and Large ($-331.58 \pm 1.71$ vs $-337.77 \pm 1.31$). On the last-100 reward
  --- the end-of-training performance --- \texttt{gt\_\allowbreak position\_\allowbreak velocity}
  leads on all four PointMaze environments. Read literally: the ground-truth bonus reaches the
  better final policy everywhere, but on Open and Large SAC $+$ RND collects more reward during
  training, so the whole-run mean currently favors it there.
\item \textbf{Every bonus arm solves every PointMaze environment; plain SAC falls behind as the
  maze grows.} SAC $+$ RND and SAC $+$ \texttt{gt\_\allowbreak position\_\allowbreak velocity}
  reach success rate $\ge 0.997$ on all four PointMaze environments. Plain SAC
  (\texttt{no\_\allowbreak exploration}) reaches $1.000$ on Open but $0.923$ on UMaze, $0.938$ on
  Medium, and $0.463$ on Large, where its whole-run reward ($-630.17 \pm 21.44$) is far behind
  the two bonus arms (about $-332$ to $-338$).
\item \textbf{On AntMaze, only SAC $+$ RND reaches the goal in a meaningful fraction of
  episodes.} Its last-100 success rate is $0.061$ (UMaze), $0.095$ (Open), $0.015$ (Medium), $0$
  (Large), with maze-cell coverage $100.00$ (UMaze, Open) and $96.95 \pm 0.87$ (Medium). The two
  ground-truth count arms and plain SAC are at success rate $\approx 0$ on every AntMaze
  environment; the count bonuses do raise coverage over plain SAC (for example Open:
  $82.56 \pm 1.48$ and $81.52 \pm 1.51$ vs $78.44 \pm 0.80$ maze-cell coverage). The bold steps-to-goal
  cells in ground-truth rows rest on a handful of successful runs (their ($n$=..) counts) and are
  not comparable to the SAC $+$ RND row. One recorded design difference to keep in mind when
  reading this gap: the two AntMaze ground-truth bonuses count positions only (the $4$~m maze
  cell or the $1$~m square of $x, y$), while the RND bonus reads the full $29$-dimensional state.
\item \textbf{AntMaze Large is unsolved by every arm so far.} Every completed run of every
  algorithm ends every episode at the $1000$-step limit (whole-run reward $-1000.00 \pm 0.00$,
  success rate $0$), and maze-cell coverage stays near $10\%$. Where every configuration of a
  cell sits at $-1000.00$ --- the three bonus cells of AntMaze Large, and the two ground-truth
  cells of AntMaze Medium --- the racing score cannot separate the configurations: nothing has
  been pruned there (those cells still race all $15$ bonus weights), and the best-configuration
  row label shown for them (bonus-weight-$0.001$) means only ``no configuration distinguishable
  yet'', not a real winner.
\item \textbf{Best bonus weights so far.} The best SAC $+$ RND bonus weight is large on every
  environment with a distinguishable best ($300$ to $10^{4}$), while the best
  \texttt{gt\_\allowbreak position\_\allowbreak velocity} weight on PointMaze is moderate ($0.3$
  to $3$).
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Training cost.}
Table~\ref{tab:pm-am-trainrun1-cost} gives the wall-clock training time per run (every run is
$10^{6}$ steps, so this is the time per million steps), split by environment family and device,
over the $14171$ completed records on disk at the 2026-08-01 count (a few more than the figure's
$14154$ --- the last collaborator workers were still finishing). jaguar03 hosted none of this
sweep's jobs, so no exclusion applies. The workers were packed many per node, so the times
measure throughput under node contention, not the environments' intrinsic step cost --- which is
why PointMaze reads slower than AntMaze here; the two families were also not spread identically
across the node classes.

\subsubsection{Train run 1.2}
\label{sec:pm-am-trainrun12}

Train run~1.1 leaves one opening on AntMaze: SAC $+$ RND is the only arm that ever reaches the goal
(finding~3 above), while the two oracle count arms and plain SAC stay at a success rate of about
zero on all four AntMaze environments. Train run~1.2 therefore drops PointMaze, drops the two
AntMaze environments on which no arm ever succeeds, keeps the two on which the RND arm did reach
the goal --- \texttt{AntMaze\_\allowbreak UMaze-v5\_\allowbreak start\_\allowbreak
bottom\_\allowbreak left} and \texttt{AntMaze\_\allowbreak Medium-v5\_\allowbreak
start\_\allowbreak bottom\_\allowbreak left} --- and tests four new RND variants against train
run~1.1's winning RND configuration on them. The environment specification is unchanged
(subsubsection~\ref{sec:pm-am-envspec}, Table~\ref{tab:pm-am-trainrun1-envs}); only the number of
env steps and the device change.

The run is two independent tasks executed as one sweep, under one run folder, one queue, and one
controller.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Task R --- the RND baseline at $10^{7}$ steps.} Train run~1.1's winning SAC $+$ RND
  configuration per environment (bonus weight $10^{4}$ on AntMaze UMaze, $3{\times}10^{3}$ on
  AntMaze Medium; the \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} stack with reward normalization on), run to
  $10^{7}$ env steps with $100$ seeds per environment and never pruned. It is the comparison curve,
  and its completed records are also the intended source of the $10^{7}$-step bar of the deferred
  second stage described below.
\item \textbf{Task S --- the four new arms at $10^{6}$ steps.} Every configuration of algorithms~1,
  2.1, 2.2 and 2.3 (Table~\ref{tab:pm-am-trainrun12-algos}), run to $10^{6}$ env steps and racing
  to $100$ seeds per configuration against a bar frozen before launch, with prune checks starting
  at $30$ completed seeds.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{The two predictor training losses.} Define $\theta$ as the RND predictor's parameters and
$e_\theta(s) \in \mathbb{R}^{128}$ as the target-minus-predictor error vector at state $s$ over the
$128$ output dimensions. Algorithms~2.1--2.3 add a third network: a copy of the predictor taken at
initialization (after the weight and bias initialization) and frozen for the whole run; define
$e_0(s)$ as the target-minus-frozen-initial-predictor error vector at the same state, read through
the same current state normalization as $e_\theta(s)$. Define $b = 256$ as the replay minibatch
size, $s_1, \ldots, s_b$ as the states of one replay minibatch, and $\delta = 10^{-8}$ as a
division guard. The two losses the predictor is trained on in this run are
\begin{align}
L_{\mathrm{original}}(\theta) &= \frac{1}{b} \sum_{i=1}^{b} \frac{1}{2}
  \lVert e_\theta(s_i) \rVert_2^2, \\
L_{\mathrm{normalized}}(\theta) &= \frac{1}{b} \sum_{i=1}^{b}
  \frac{\lVert e_\theta(s_i) \rVert_2^2}{\lVert e_0(s_i) \rVert_2^2 + \delta}.
\end{align}
Both average over the minibatch and sum over the $128$ output dimensions. The normalized loss
divides each sample's squared error by that sample's frozen-initialization squared error --- a
per-sample constant that carries no gradient --- so a state with a large initial error is
down-weighted in proportion to it, and at update $0$ the predictor still equals its frozen copy, so
$L_{\mathrm{normalized}} \approx 1$. The normalized form carries no factor $\tfrac{1}{2}$; that
missing factor is a pure constant which only doubles the gradient, so it is equivalent to doubling
the learning rate and is absorbed by the learning-rate sweep. $L_{\mathrm{original}}$ is the loss
train run~1.1 used, and here it is used by the RND baseline and by algorithms~1, 2.1 and 2.3;
$L_{\mathrm{normalized}}$ is used by algorithm~2.2 alone.

\paragraph{The ratio bonus.} Define $\varepsilon = 10^{-8}$ as a division guard on the bonus side
and $B(s)$ as the intrinsic bonus an arm hands to SAC at state $s$ (entering the replay buffer as
extrinsic $+\ \beta \times B$, the same pipeline as train run~1.1). Algorithm~1 reads the plain
error norm, $B(s) = \lVert e_\theta(s) \rVert_2$; algorithms~2.1, 2.2 and 2.3 read the ratio of the
two error norms,
\begin{align}
B(s) = \frac{\lVert e_\theta(s) \rVert_2}{\lVert e_0(s) \rVert_2 + \varepsilon}.
\end{align}
At initialization the predictor is an exact copy of the frozen network, so $e_\theta = e_0$ and
$B(s) = 1$ at every state: the ratio bonus starts flat whatever the initial error field looks like,
which is the property the three algorithm-2 arms are built to test. The bonus readout never enters
the training loss, in any arm.

\paragraph{Truncation: stage-1 racing now, stage~2 deferred.} Define $\bar{R}$ as a run's whole-run
mean training return (the mean of its per-episode extrinsic returns over all of its completed
training episodes) --- the score train run~1.1 raced on, metric~1 of the list above --- $n$ as the
number of completed runs (seeds) of one configuration, and $s_{\bar{R}}$ as the sample standard
deviation of $\bar{R}$ over those $n$ runs. Only records a run wrote at its natural end
(\texttt{completed=true}) enter any of these three quantities. The rules are:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{The bar is frozen before launch.} Define $\bar{R}^{\star}_{\mathrm{env}}$ as the bar
  of environment $\mathrm{env}$: the mean $\bar{R}$ of train run~1.1's winning SAC $+$ RND
  configuration at $10^{6}$ steps. The launch recompute over ALL completed train-run-1.1 records
  froze $-689.74$ on AntMaze UMaze ($n=84$) and $-998.47$ on AntMaze Medium ($n=82$) --- slightly
  different from Table~\ref{tab:pm-am-run1-interim-antmaze}'s $-689.85$ / $-998.48$, which is the
  earlier $n=77$ snapshot. The frozen values live in \texttt{slurm/FROZEN\_\allowbreak BARS.json}
  in the run folder, committed and never regenerated; every decision line records the file's hash.
\item \textbf{Prune rule.} A configuration with $n \ge 30$ completed runs is pruned at the first
  controller tick at which its $99\%$ upper confidence bound falls below its environment's bar,
  that is $\bar{R} + 2.576\,s_{\bar{R}}/\sqrt{n} < \bar{R}^{\star}_{\mathrm{env}}$. The check is
  repeated every tick as $n$ grows towards $100$, so a configuration can be pruned at any $n$
  between $30$ and $100$. A pruned configuration receives no new seeds: its remaining seed markers
  are archived, while its already-running seeds finish and are kept.
\item \textbf{Survivor.} A configuration that reaches $100$ completed runs without ever being
  pruned is a stage-1 survivor. That verdict is final and is written to the decision log, and it is
  the input the deferred second stage would consume.
\item \textbf{Task R is exempt.} The RND baseline is never pruned and never scored against the bar;
  it is the benchmark the bar was computed from, and it runs its $100$ seeds per environment to
  $10^{7}$ steps regardless of what task~S does.
\end{enumerate}
A second stage --- carrying the stage-1 survivors to $10^{7}$ steps and pruning one only when it
fails both the $10^{6}$-step bar above and a $10^{7}$-step bar computed from task~R's completed
records --- is designed but deliberately not implemented in this wave; that is also why this run
writes no model checkpoints.

configuration that beat the RND benchmark on fresh seeds in train run~3.2.4 --- carried over to
AntMaze. That family and the RND arm of train run~1.1 differ in many knobs at once, so
Table~\ref{tab:pm-am-trainrun12-rank1-vs-rnd} lists the differences knob by knob, to make explicit
which side of each difference train run~1.2's algorithm~1 takes.

\end{quote}
\end{samepage}
\noindent with the layout every train run in this
document uses: \texttt{experiment\_\allowbreak background.md} for the launch record,
\texttt{slurm/} for the queue, the worker scripts, the frozen bars and the controller,
\texttt{data/} for one JSON record per run, and \texttt{analysis/} for the results. Task~R's
workers run on the gpu partition with the cuda device and on the gnolim partition with the cpu
device --- gnolim's Pascal cards cannot run this environment's torch build, as train run~1.1
verified --- and a gnolim worker claims $10^{7}$-step items first, falling back to $10^{6}$-step
items when no $10^{7}$-step item is claimable. Task~S's workers are cpu-only, on the cpu and nolim
partitions and on the reserved jaguar03 node used as a CPU server. No model checkpoints are
written: every run completes inside one Slurm job, and a run killed mid-flight is re-queued and
re-run from the start, exactly as in train run~1.1.

\subsubsection{Why the AntMaze runs do not converge}
\label{sec:pm-am-nonconvergence}

\claudeedit{Every reward curve of this section plateaus far below solving --- and the task-R
baseline, given $10^{7}$ steps, ends WORSE ($-695.29$) than the same configuration's own
$10^{6}$-step value in train run~1.1 ($-689.85$). This subsubsection explains why, from the runs'
own records (three sweeps, $23{,}045$ completed records; extraction and every number regenerable
from \texttt{analysis/\allowbreak 2026-08-08-01-55\_\allowbreak antmaze-nonconvergence/} in this
run folder). Define the per-block success rate $\mathrm{SR}$ as the number of goal-reaching
episodes over the number of episodes in a block of training steps. The failure has two distinct
shapes. At $10^{6}$ steps, on every arm of every sweep, the goal is touched but never becomes
policy: median whole-run success is $\approx 0$ for every configuration, while best single
episodes ($-46$ to $-148$) show the goal is reachable. At $10^{7}$ steps
(Figure~\ref{fig:pm-am-nonconv-phenomenon}) the goal IS found and then UNLEARNED: per-1M-block
median $\mathrm{SR}$ on UMaze runs $0.033,\ 0.059,\ 0.028,\ 0.004,\ 0.002,\ 0.0007$, then exactly
$0$ for blocks 7--10 (Medium: rises to $\approx 0.02$ by block 4, exactly $0$ from block 8) ---
while maze-cell coverage is $100\%$ from a median of $0.05$M steps (UMaze) / $0.45$M (Medium)
onward. Every UMaze seed succeeds at least once; the median LAST success is at $5.56$M steps, and
nothing succeeds after $\approx 7$M.}

\claudeedit{The strongest mechanism is credit assignment at this discount
(Figure~\ref{fig:pm-am-nonconv-credit}). Define $T$ as the episode cap, $k$ as the step at which a
successful episode terminates, and $\gamma = 0.99$ the discount; a success improves the
start-state value over a timeout by $\Delta_\gamma = (\gamma^{k} - \gamma^{T})/(1-\gamma)$,
against a return scale of $(1-\gamma^{T})/(1-\gamma) \approx 100$. The measured median $k$ is
$462$ (UMaze, of cap $700$) and $752$ (Medium, of $1000$) --- so $\Delta_\gamma$ is $0.87$ (0.9\%
of the scale) and $0.048$ (0.05\%). On the PointMaze UMaze control of train run~1.1 --- same code,
same $-1$/$0$ reward, same $\gamma$, and it converges --- the median $k$ is $105$ and
$\Delta_\gamma = 29.9$, i.e.\ $31\%$ of the scale: the signal a success carries is $34$--$620\times$
weaker on AntMaze. Second, the objective never becomes the task: the median per-block ratio of the
$\beta$-scaled intrinsic sum to the extrinsic sum on the task-R baseline (reward normalization ON)
falls only from $3.3$ to $1.7$ across the ten 1M blocks on UMaze and stays $\approx 1$ on Medium
--- after $10^{7}$ steps, with nothing left to explore, the bonus still matches or outweighs the
task; on the reward-norm-OFF task-S arms the ratio at the winning weights is $10$--$10^{3}$. A
fixed goal-reaching policy is not a fixed point of that objective. Third, the successes are
stumbled upon, not learned: their steps-to-goal barely moves over training (first-half median
$488$, second-half $436$, against the $\approx 191$ the same runs' best episodes demonstrate), so
they are exploration by-products. Fourth --- a lock-in rather than a trigger --- the replay buffer
holds $10^{6}$ transitions (the never-overridden SB3 default) on a $10^{7}$-step run:
$\mathrm{SR}$ halves while the buffer still holds $\approx 41$ successful episodes, so eviction
does not start the decay, but once the last success is evicted no gradient update ever sees a goal
transition again, the extrinsic target becomes a constant $-1$ per step, and recovery is
impossible. Fifth, the terminal set is small and episodes can be burned: the $0.45$~m goal radius
is hard-coded while AntMaze cells are $4$~m ($\approx 4\%$ of a cell terminates, against
$\approx 64\%$ on PointMaze), and the maze wrapper discards the inner Ant's termination flag, so a
flipped ant runs out the episode at $-1$ per step with no reset.}

\claudeedit{Three things the failure is NOT. It is not exploration: coverage is complete within
the first half-million steps and stays complete while success decays to zero. It is not the
quality of the RND bonus: RND is the BEST explorer in train run~1.1 (winner coverage $100\%$),
while the ground-truth count arms explore WORSE there (winner coverage $64$--$69\%$ --- a count
bonus saturates as counts grow) and score exactly zero success; no bonus in the sweep converts
complete coverage into a policy. And it is not the step budget: ten times the steps made the final
policy worse. The learning-rate addendum fits this account without contradicting it: raising the
predictor rate raises success and shortens paths monotonically (UMaze whole-run success $0.133$ at
Adam $10^{-2}$ vs.\ $0.066$ at $10^{-3}$ vs.\ $0.061$ at $10^{-4}$; median $k$ $376$ vs.\ $476$
vs.\ $\approx 449$) --- consistent with a faster predictor acting as an implicit bonus anneal ---
but $13\%$ success at $k \approx 376$ of a $700$-step cap is still far from convergence.}

\claudeedit{Context and levers. The paper this environment choice follows (ExPLORe) does not run
online SAC from scratch: it runs RLPD --- SAC with a $50/50$ replay mix of OFFLINE goal-reaching
prior data and $20$ gradient steps per environment step --- where these runs use SB3 SAC, one
environment, one gradient step per step, and no prior data (the design-difference rows are in the
train-run-5 comparison table). Ranked by the mechanism each targets: raise $\gamma$ to $0.999$ or
use multi-step returns (moves UMaze's $\Delta_\gamma$ share from $0.9\%$ to $\approx 55\%$ at the
observed $k$); anneal the bonus, or pair a smaller bonus weight with the faster predictor rate;
size the buffer to the run ($10^{7}$ Ant transitions $\approx 5$--$6$~GB); take more than one
gradient step per environment step; add a deterministic standalone evaluation to separate the
exploration policy from what the critic could exploit; and log trajectories or visit maps so the
flipped-ant cost becomes measurable.}
\claudecomment{Sources: train run 1.1 ($14{,}488$ completed 1M-step records), train run 1.2
($6{,}254$ completed: the four task-S arms at 1M and the task-R baseline at 10M, $n=17$ UMaze /
$20$ Medium — final, the sweep stopped 2026-08-05), and the learning-rate addendum ($2{,}303$
completed, final at the 2026-08-08 stop; its Adam $10^{-2}$ numbers rest on $11$--$12$ seeds). Extraction
one-pass with \texttt{json.load} per record; verification gates reproduce run-1.1's published
success rates ($0.0619$ vs $0.061$ UMaze $n=84$; $0.0152$ vs $0.015$ Medium $n=82$), the task-R
rewards to the cent ($-695.29$/$-996.75$), and the reward identity (success at step $k$ earns
$-(k-1)$, a timeout earns $-T$) with $0$ violations over all $20{,}742$ shift($-1$) records. The
success rate, steps-to-goal $k$, and $\Delta_\gamma$ are NEW metrics introduced here; the last-100
success-rate definition is the one train run 1.1's monitoring used, so those two sources cannot
drift. Ratios of intrinsic to extrinsic sums are medians over episodes because a reward-norm-ON
run's first episodes have ratios $\sim 10^{3}$ (a warm-up artifact that would dominate a mean),
and reward-norm-ON arms are not comparable to reward-norm-OFF arms on this metric. The ExPLORe
sentences re-present facts already tabulated in the train-run-5 section and add no new claim about
that paper's results; no new citation is added here.}

\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.98\linewidth]{../train_runs/2026-08-01-01-44_run_8_1_2_antmaze-umaze-medium-bottom-left_sac__rnd-origsmall-beta1e4-3e3-10M-100seed__alg1-sgd-l2-biasnormal0.5__alg2.1-ratio__alg2.2-normloss__alg2.3-layernorm__lr-1e-3-1e-2_beta-1e-3-to-1e4-x15_1M-prune30-race100-frozenbar_gamma0.99/analysis/2026-08-08-01-55_antmaze-nonconvergence/plots/f1_phenomenon.pdf}
\caption{\claudeedit{The phenomenon: the task-R baseline finds the goal and then unlearns it while
exploration saturates. One column per environment; top row: per-seed success rate per 1M-step
block (gray) with the across-seed median (black); bottom row: median maze-cell and 1~m coverage
(blue, left axis) against the same median success rate (black, right axis). Coverage is $100\%$
from $0.05$M steps (UMaze) / $0.45$M (Medium) while the success rate decays to exactly zero.
Generated by \texttt{analysis/\allowbreak 2026-08-08-01-55\_\allowbreak
antmaze-nonconvergence/\allowbreak code/\allowbreak make\_\allowbreak figures.py}.}}
\label{fig:pm-am-nonconv-phenomenon}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.98\linewidth]{../train_runs/2026-08-01-01-44_run_8_1_2_antmaze-umaze-medium-bottom-left_sac__rnd-origsmall-beta1e4-3e3-10M-100seed__alg1-sgd-l2-biasnormal0.5__alg2.1-ratio__alg2.2-normloss__alg2.3-layernorm__lr-1e-3-1e-2_beta-1e-3-to-1e4-x15_1M-prune30-race100-frozenbar_gamma0.99/analysis/2026-08-08-01-55_antmaze-nonconvergence/plots/f4_credit.pdf}
\caption{\claudeedit{The mechanism: where successes happen vs.\ what they are worth. Left:
distribution of steps-to-goal $k$ over every successful episode --- the PointMaze UMaze control's
successes cluster at the $1/(1-\gamma) = 100$ horizon, while the AntMaze successes happen at
$400$--$1000$ steps. Right: the start-state advantage $\Delta_\gamma = (\gamma^{k} -
\gamma^{T})/(1-\gamma)$ at each environment's median $k$, log scale, against the $\approx 100$
return scale: $29.9$ on the converging control, $0.87$ and $0.048$ on the AntMaze pair. Same
generator as Figure~\ref{fig:pm-am-nonconv-phenomenon}.}}
\label{fig:pm-am-nonconv-credit}
\end{figure}

% Section 9 draft — CleanRL PPO + RND on Atari, train run 1.
% Spliced into RND_development_document.tex before \section{Distance to ground truth metrics}.
% Figure: code/2026-08-05_cleanrl-rnd-writeup/rnd_pipeline_comparison.pdf (standalone TikZ,

\clearpage
\section{CleanRL PPO \texorpdfstring{$+$}{+} RND on Atari}
\label{sec:cleanrl}

Every RND result so far in this document comes from one implementation --- the project's own, built
up over train runs 1 through 5 and reused unchanged by \Cref{sec:pm-am}. That implementation was
written against the original paper and the original repository, but it has never been checked against
a second, independently written RND. This section runs one: CleanRL's
\texttt{ppo\_\allowbreak rnd\_\allowbreak envpool.py}, a widely used single-file PPO $+$ RND on
Atari, at the upstream commit \texttt{fe8d8a0} (2026-04-20).

Two things make it worth the compute. First, it is a different RND on a different domain with a
different base learner --- pixels instead of coordinates, PPO instead of SAC, two value heads instead
of one reward stream --- so agreement or disagreement with our own RND is informative in a way that
another sweep of our own code is not. Second, its published result is a single seed. CleanRL reports
$7100$ on MontezumaRevenge against Burda et al.'s $8152$, and states plainly that one seed was all
the compute allowed. Thirty seeds turn that single number into a distribution.

\subsection{Train run 1}
\label{sec:cleanrl-trainrun1}

This run asks three questions.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Does the published single-seed result hold up across seeds?} CleanRL's $7100$ is one
  sample from a distribution whose spread is unknown. Thirty seeds give a mean and a spread to
  compare against Burda et al.'s three-seed $8152$.
\item \textbf{What does the environment auto-reset defect cost?} CleanRL's own documentation records
  a bug in this file and says ``it does not seem to impact performance'', without evidence.
  \Cref{sec:cleanrl-bug} shows the defect is larger than the documentation implies --- it
  contaminates the intrinsic advantage of every preceding step in a rollout, not just the affected
  row --- and this run uses the corrected code.
\item \textbf{Where do the two RND implementations actually differ?}
  \Cref{tab:cleanrl-vs-project-rnd} and \Cref{fig:cleanrl-rnd-pipelines} answer this knob by knob.
  Several of the differences are ones this project deliberately chose against CleanRL, and having
  both stacks written down in one place is a prerequisite for attributing any result to any of them.
\end{enumerate}

% --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
% (1) The comparison table, then the comparison figure.
% --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{The two RND implementations, knob by knob.}
\Cref{tab:cleanrl-vs-project-rnd} lists every load-bearing design choice of the two stacks side by
side. The right-hand column is the \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} RND, which is the RND of
\Cref{tab:pm-am-trainrun1-algos} and of every RND arm in \Cref{sec:pm-am}; its cell-by-cell
provenance is \Cref{tab:trainrun5-hparams}. Rows where the two agree are marked \emph{same}; the
remaining rows are the whole of the difference between the two implementations.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.4cm}
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{5.6cm}
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{5.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Knob} &
\textbf{CleanRL PPO $+$ RND} &
\textbf{This project's SAC $+$ RND} \\
& \small(\texttt{ppo\_\allowbreak rnd\_\allowbreak envpool.py} @ \texttt{fe8d8a0}) &
  \small(\runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$}, \Cref{tab:pm-am-trainrun1-algos}) \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\textit{Base learner and environment}} \\
base RL algorithm & PPO, on-policy, $128$ parallel environment copies & SAC, off-policy, one
  environment copy, replay buffer $10^{6}$ \\
domain & Atari from pixels: \texttt{Montezuma\allowbreak Revenge-v5} via envpool & PointMaze and
  AntMaze from coordinates \\
observation the bonus sees & newest frame of a $4\times84\times84$ \texttt{uint8} stack, so
  $1\times84\times84$ & the full state vector: $4$-d PointMaze, $29$-d AntMaze \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\textit{Bonus input}} \\
what the bonus is computed on & next observation $s'$ & same \\
whitening & running mean/std per pixel, clip $[-5,5]$, denominator $\sqrt{\sigma^2}$ & running
  mean/std per dimension, clip $[-5,5]$, denominator $\sqrt{\sigma^2+10^{-8}}$ \\
warm-up before training & $50 \times 128 = 6400$ rollout steps of a random agent, on the
  \emph{training} environments & $6400$ environment steps of a random agent, on a \emph{fresh
  throwaway} environment so the training visit counts stay clean \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\textit{Networks}} \\
target network & $3$ convolutions ($32,64,64$) $\to$ flatten $\to$ linear $512$; frozen & linear
  $4\to256\to128$; frozen \\
predictor network & the same $3$ convolutions $\to$ linear $512 \to 512 \to 512$, i.e.\ \textbf{two}
  fully-connected blocks deeper than the target & the same trunk \textbf{one} $128$-wide block
  deeper: $4\to256\to128\to128$ \\
output width $m$ & $512$ & $128$ \\
activation & LeakyReLU on the convolutions, PyTorch default negative slope $0.01$; ReLU on the
  predictor's extra blocks & LeakyReLU throughout, negative slope $0.2$ (the TensorFlow default the
  original paper used) \\
weight initialization & orthogonal, gain $\sqrt2$ & same \\
bias initialization & zero & same \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\textit{Readout and predictor training}} \\
bonus readout & $b = \tfrac12\sum_{j=1}^{m} e_j^2$ (half sum over $512$ dimensions) & $b =
  \tfrac1m\sum_{j=1}^{m} e_j^2$ (mean over $128$ dimensions) \\
predictor loss & squared error, mean over the output width, then masked and averaged over the
  minibatch & squared error, mean over the output width, averaged over the replay batch \\
update proportion & $0.25$ --- a random quarter of each minibatch trains the predictor, following
  the paper's Table 5 & $1.0$ --- the whole batch trains the predictor, following the original
  repository code \\
predictor input at update time & the \emph{stored current} observation of the rollout, not the next
  observation the bonus was computed on & the next observation, the same input the bonus used \\
optimizer & Adam, learning rate $10^{-4}$, $\epsilon = 10^{-5}$, \textbf{shared with the policy};
  gradients clipped at global norm $0.5$ & Adam, learning rate $10^{-4}$, $\epsilon = 10^{-8}$,
  \textbf{its own optimizer}; no gradient clipping \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\textit{Intrinsic reward}} \\
reward normalization & divide by the running standard deviation $\sigma_F$ of a non-episodic forward
  filter $F_t = \gamma_{\mathrm{int}} F_{t-1} + b$, no mean subtracted & same mechanism, same
  $\gamma = 0.99$ \\
forward-filter discount & $\gamma_{\mathrm{int}} = 0.99$ & $0.99$ \\
how the bonus enters the objective & a \textbf{second value head} with its own non-episodic return
  at $\gamma = 0.99$; the two advantages are combined as $A = 1.0\,A^{\mathrm{int}} +
  2.0\,A^{\mathrm{ext}}$ & a \textbf{single reward stream}: the replay buffer stores $r^{\mathrm{ext}}
  + \beta\,r^{\mathrm{int}}$, so there is no intrinsic value head \\
episodic treatment of the bonus & the intrinsic return never resets at an episode boundary & the
  bonus is inside the one reward, so it inherits SAC's episodic treatment \\
bonus weight & fixed by $\mathtt{int\_coef}=1.0$ and $\mathtt{ext\_coef}=2.0$; not swept & $\beta$
  swept over $15$ values from $10^{-3}$ to $10^{4}$ \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\textit{Run scale}} \\
environment steps per run & $2\times10^{9}$ & $10^{6}$ \\
seeds & $1$ published; $30$ in this run & $100$ to $300$ per arm \\
discount on the extrinsic stream & $0.999$ & $0.99$ in \Cref{sec:pm-am} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}%
}
\caption{The two RND implementations knob by knob. The left column is CleanRL's
\texttt{ppo\_\allowbreak rnd\_\allowbreak envpool.py} at commit \texttt{fe8d8a0}, read from the
source; the right column is the \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} stack, whose per-cell provenance is
\Cref{tab:trainrun5-hparams}. ``same'' means the two implementations agree on that row.
\Cref{fig:cleanrl-rnd-pipelines} draws the same comparison as two pipelines. Four of these
differences are ones this project chose deliberately against CleanRL and recorded at the time: the
LeakyReLU slope of $0.2$ rather than $0.01$, the mean readout rather than the half sum, the update
proportion of $1.0$ rather than $0.25$, and feeding the predictor's update the next observation
rather than the stored current one.}
\label{tab:cleanrl-vs-project-rnd}
\end{table}

\Cref{fig:cleanrl-rnd-pipelines} draws the same two stacks as pipelines, read top to bottom, with the
rows aligned so that a horizontal line across the page compares the same stage in both. Stages the
two implementations share are gray; stages that differ are orange, with the difference written into
the box. The figure is drawn vertically and at the document's own text width, so its text prints at
full body size --- \Cref{fig:trainrun5-pipeline}, the horizontal pipeline of train run 5, is
$11.1$~inches wide and is therefore scaled to about half when included, which is why its labels are
hard to read.

% --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Environment specification.}
The environment is a single Atari game, \texttt{Montezuma\allowbreak Revenge-v5}, built by envpool
$0.6.6$ with four explicit keyword arguments and seventeen defaults. Because almost all of the
preprocessing lives inside envpool's C++ rather than in a stack of visible Python wrappers,
\Cref{tab:cleanrl-envspec} lists every configuration key that is in force and says where each value
comes from, so the environment can be rebuilt from the table alone.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{4.2cm}
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{5.0cm}
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{5.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} &
\textbf{Value} &
\textbf{Where the value comes from} \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\textit{Identity and spaces}} \\
environment id & \texttt{Montezuma\allowbreak Revenge-v5} & passed by CleanRL; the benchmark script's
  only environment \\
task and ROM & \texttt{montezuma\_\allowbreak revenge}, ROM shipped inside the envpool wheel &
  derived from the id; the ROM ships inside the envpool wheel \\
observation space & \texttt{Box(0, 255, (4, 84, 84), uint8)} & envpool defaults
  \texttt{stack\_num=4}, \texttt{img\_height=\allowbreak img\_width=84},
  \texttt{gray\_scale=True} \\
channel order & oldest frame first, so index $3$ is the most recent frame & envpool layout; the RND
  bonus uses index $3$ only \\
action space & \texttt{Discrete(18)} & the ALE minimal action set, which for this game is the full
  set \\
parallel copies & $128$ & \texttt{num\_envs}, passed \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\textit{Preprocessing}} \\
frame skip & $4$ emulator frames per agent step, rewards summed & envpool default
  \texttt{frame\_skip=4} \\
max-pooling & element-wise maximum of the last two of the four skipped frames & envpool, fixed \\
grayscale and resize & ALE palette grayscale, then $210\times160 \to 84\times84$ with OpenCV
  \texttt{INTER\_AREA} & envpool defaults \texttt{gray\_scale=True},
  \texttt{use\_inter\_area\_resize=True} \\
no-ops at reset & uniform on $\{0,\dots,29\}$, then one FIRE frame & envpool default
  \texttt{noop\_max=30}; FIRE is unconditional in $0.6.6$ when FIRE is in the minimal action set \\
sticky actions & repeat-action probability $0.25$, applied by the ALE itself & passed by CleanRL,
  matching the original RND paper \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\textit{Episode}} \\
episodic life & \texttt{done} is raised on every life lost, not only on game over & passed
  (\texttt{episodic\_life=True}); the underlying game is not reset on a life loss \\
max episode steps & $108{,}000$ agent steps, so $432{,}000$ emulator frames & envpool's
  \texttt{registration.py} for every \texttt{-v5} id; in practice non-binding \\
termination rule & game over, or the step cap, or a life lost & envpool \texttt{atari\_env.h} \\
auto-reset & next-step: the terminating step returns the terminal observation, and the following
  step is consumed by the reset & envpool, fixed --- this is the defect
  \Cref{sec:cleanrl-bug} corrects \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\textit{Reward}} \\
reward seen by the agent & clipped to its sign, so $\{-1,0,+1\}$ & passed
  (\texttt{reward\_clip=True}) \\
reward recorded for scoring & the raw unclipped game score, kept in \texttt{info["reward"]} & envpool
  preserves it; this is what the episodic return in the run record accumulates \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}%
}
\caption{Environment specification for train run~1, in the style of \Cref{tab:pm-am-envspec}. Every
envpool configuration key in force is listed with its origin: ``passed'' means CleanRL supplies it
explicitly in \texttt{envpool.make}, everything else is an envpool default or registration value.
Two values are worth care and were verified against the installed $0.6.6$ rather than the web
documentation: \texttt{max\_\allowbreak episode\_\allowbreak steps} is $108{,}000$ \emph{agent}
steps (the web table's $27{,}000$ is a newer release's default), and the keys
\texttt{use\_\allowbreak fire\_\allowbreak reset} and \texttt{full\_\allowbreak action\_\allowbreak
space} do not exist in $0.6.6$ at all.}
\label{tab:cleanrl-envspec}
\end{table}

\paragraph{The five arms.}
The run is an ablation of three of CleanRL's choices, each of which this project's own RND makes
differently. \Cref{tab:cleanrl-arms} defines the arms. Arm~1 is the reference and arms~2 to~4 each
change one knob from it; arm~5 changes all three at once, so the difference between arm~5 and the sum
of arms~2 to~4 measures whether the three interact. Every other value --- the hyperparameters of
\Cref{tab:cleanrl-trainrun1-algos}, the environment of \Cref{tab:cleanrl-envspec}, and the auto-reset
correction of \Cref{sec:cleanrl-bug} --- is identical across all five, so an arm-to-arm difference can
only come from the marked knobs.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.95cm}
>{\centering\arraybackslash}p{1.9cm}
>{\centering\arraybackslash}p{1.9cm}
>{\centering\arraybackslash}p{1.9cm}
>{\centering\arraybackslash}p{1.9cm}
>{\centering\arraybackslash}p{1.9cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Knob} & \textbf{Arm 1} & \textbf{Arm 2} & \textbf{Arm 3} & \textbf{Arm 4} & \textbf{Arm 5} \\
& \small(original) & \small(no RND clip) & \small(full update) & \small(shallower) & \small(all three) \\
\midrule
policy gradient clipping, global norm & $0.5$ & $0.5$ & $0.5$ & $0.5$ & $0.5$ \\
RND predictor gradient clipping & joint & \criticalknob{none} & joint & joint & \criticalknob{none} \\
predictor update proportion & $0.25$ & $0.25$ & \criticalknob{$1.0$} & $0.25$ & \criticalknob{$1.0$} \\
predictor blocks beyond the target & $2$ & $2$ & $2$ & \criticalknob{$1$} & \criticalknob{$1$} \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{6}{@{}l}{\textit{What each departure moves toward}} \\
matches this project's RND & N/A & \cmark\ (no predictor clip) & \cmark\ (proportion $1.0$) &
  \cmark\ (one block deeper) & \cmark\ (all three) \\
seeds & $30$ & $30$ & $30$ & $30$ & $30$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{The five arms of train run~1. Blue marks the knob each arm changes from arm~1. The
\emph{policy} is clipped at a global norm of $0.5$ in every arm, including arms~2 and~5: what those
two arms remove is the clipping of the RND predictor alone. ``joint'' means the predictor is clipped
through the same single global-norm call as the policy, which is what CleanRL does --- one
\texttt{clip\_grad\_norm\_} over both parameter lists, so the predictor's gradient is scaled whenever
the two networks' combined norm exceeds $0.5$, whether or not the predictor caused the excess.
Removing the predictor from that call therefore also changes what the policy is clipped on, from the
combined norm to its own. All three
departures move CleanRL's RND toward the \runref{run-5 original RND, Adam $10^{-4}$} stack of
\Cref{tab:cleanrl-vs-project-rnd}: that stack does not clip the predictor's gradient, trains the
predictor on the whole batch rather than a random quarter, and makes its predictor one block deeper
than its target rather than two. So arm~5 is the closest CleanRL's implementation gets to this
project's without changing the base learner or the domain. ``N/A'' marks a row that does not apply to
a column. Each arm runs $30$ seeds; the run identifiers are ordered seed-outermost and arm-inner, so a
campaign stopped early still holds every arm at the same seed count.}
\label{tab:cleanrl-arms}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Where the arms stand at the halfway point.}
The campaign is unfinished, so no run has a final score and neither artifact below can use a run's
last logged row: a run's last step reflects the speed of the node it landed on as much as its arm, and
reading arms at different depths would rank node allocations rather than algorithms. Both therefore
fix the step first and read every arm at that same step. \Cref{tab:cleanrl-interim} reports one number
per arm --- the extrinsic reward at $10^{9}$ steps, half of the $2\times10^{9}$ planned --- and
\Cref{fig:cleanrl-interim-curves} plots the whole trajectory up to that point and a little beyond.
When the campaign finishes, the table's step becomes the full $2\times10^{9}$ and nothing else about
it changes.

\centering
\footnotesize

\midrule
Arm 1 original & 18 & 6788.4 $\pm$ 794.5 & 6928.5 & 13494.0 \\
Arm 2 no RND clip & 17 & 6799.0 $\pm$ 618.8 & 6852.0 & 10951.5 \\
Arm 3 full batch & 18 & \textbf{7419.9 $\pm$ 725.3} & 7080.0 & 14491.0 \\
Arm 4 shallower & 22 & 5886.4 $\pm$ 581.6 & 6385.5 & 11230.5 \\
Arm 5 all three & 18 & \underline{7218.8 $\pm$ 563.8} & 7045.5 & 12350.0 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Extrinsic reward at $10^{9}$ environment steps, half of each run's $2\times10^{9}$ target,
for the five arms of train run~1. The quantity is the trailing mean over each run's last $200$
finished episodes; the seeds column counts the runs that had reached $10^{9}$ steps at the time of
reading, and the mean, its standard error, the median and the best seed are taken across those runs.
Logging is every $200$ policy updates, or $3{,}276{,}800$ steps, so no row falls exactly on $10^{9}$
and the row used is the last at or before it. Bold marks the highest mean and underline the second
highest, but the five arms lie within about two standard errors of one another and the ordering
should not be read as a result --- see the discussion below.}
\label{tab:cleanrl-interim}
\end{table}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.92\linewidth]{../../08_cleanrl_ppo_rnd/train_runs/2026-08-05-22-30_cleanrl_run_2_ablation_ppo-rnd_montezuma-v5__arm1-orig_arm2-noclip_arm3-prop1_arm4-shallow_arm5-all__2e9step_seed1-30__log200_gradstats_ckpt1h__gpu-gnolim-resv/analysis/plots/interim_arm_curves.pdf}
\caption{Extrinsic reward against environment steps for the five arms, each curve the mean across
that arm's seeds with a shaded standard error. \textbf{A curve is drawn only while at least five of
its seeds have reached that step}, and the lower panel gives the seed count behind every point, so the
truncation is visible rather than implied: past roughly $1.0\times10^{9}$ steps the count falls from
about twenty to five and the curves become correspondingly noisy. The dashed vertical line marks the
step \Cref{tab:cleanrl-interim} reads, so the table and the figure are the same measurement.}
\label{fig:cleanrl-interim-curves}
\end{figure}

Three things in these artifacts are worth stating, and only the third is about the arms.

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{All five arms learn the task.} Every arm reaches a trailing mean between $5{,}900$ and
  $7{,}400$ by $10^{9}$ steps, against the $400$ that one room's key and door pay. So the
  reproduction works, and the auto-reset correction of \Cref{sec:cleanrl-bug} did not break it.
\item \textbf{The curves have flattened since the early phase.} At $2\times10^{7}$ steps the two arms
  that leave the RND predictor unclipped led every other arm on the fraction of seeds that had scored
  at all. That gap has closed: what it measured was how quickly a seed found its first reward, not how
  much reward it eventually collects, and by $10^{9}$ steps almost every seed in every arm has found
  it. An early separation on a sparse-reward task is a separation in first-reward timing.
\item \textbf{The arms are not separated at $10^{9}$ steps.} The spread between the highest and lowest
  mean is $1{,}533$ while the standard errors are $560$ to $795$, so every pair overlaps. The one
  difference that does stand out is not in the mean: arm~5's worst seed scores $4{,}584$ while arms~1
  and~4 each have a seed at $400$, one room's worth. Whether that is a real difference in reliability
  or the luck of eighteen seeds is exactly what the remaining half of the campaign is for.
\end{enumerate}

% --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
% (4) The bug.
% --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{The environment auto-reset defect, and what it costs}
\label{sec:cleanrl-bug}

CleanRL's documentation page for this file carries a \texttt{bug} admonition. It states that envpool
and gym's vectorized environments disagree about which observation a terminating step returns, that
this makes the terminal state ``off by one'', and that ``it does not seem to impact performance, so
we take a note here and await for the upstream fix''. Two of those three claims need revising.

\paragraph{What envpool actually does.}
Define $s_{\mathrm{last}}$ as the final observation of an episode and $s_{\mathrm{new}}$ as the first
observation of the next one. envpool resets on the \emph{following} step: the step that ends an
episode returns $s_{\mathrm{last}}$ with \texttt{done=True}, and the next call to \texttt{step} is
consumed entirely by the reset, returning $s_{\mathrm{new}}$ with the submitted action discarded.
Measured directly on the installed envpool $0.6.6$ rather than taken from the documentation, on a
single CartPole copy where a reset state and a terminal state are told apart by their numbers alone:

\begin{quote}\ttfamily\footnotesize\raggedright
t=15\ \ done=True\ \ \ obs=TERMINAL\ \ \ \ elapsed\_step=16\\
t=16\ \ done=False\ \ obs=RESET-LIKE\ \ elapsed\_step=0
\end{quote}

and on \texttt{Montezuma\allowbreak Revenge-v5} with this run's exact options, where a lost life
raises \texttt{done} without resetting the game:

\begin{quote}\ttfamily\footnotesize\raggedright
t=457\ \ done=True\ \ \ lives=5\ \ elapsed\_step=458\\
t=458\ \ done=False\ \ lives=5\ \ elapsed\_step=458\ \ \ (the counter does not advance)
\end{quote}

A differential test settles that the discarded action really is discarded: two identically seeded
environment pools fed identical actions except on that one step stay byte-identical, while the same
injection one step earlier diverges immediately.

\paragraph{What that does to the rollout buffer.}
CleanRL stores \texttt{dones[step]} from the \emph{previous} iteration's \texttt{done}, so the
condition $\texttt{dones}[t]=1$ marks exactly the consumed steps. At such an index the buffer holds
an observation that is the old episode's terminal state, an action the environment threw away, a
reward that is always exactly zero, and an intrinsic bonus computed on the \emph{new} episode's first
frame. It is a transition that never happened, and it enters the policy gradient, both value losses,
the predictor's own loss, and the statistics that normalize the intrinsic reward. On
\texttt{Montezuma\allowbreak Revenge-v5} with \texttt{episodic\_life=True} it is about $1.2\%$ of
rows.

\paragraph{Why the damage is larger than that fraction suggests.}
The extrinsic stream repairs itself: at index $t-1$ the factor $1 - \texttt{dones}[t]$ is zero, so
the fabricated advantage cannot propagate backwards. The intrinsic stream has no such factor. The
intrinsic return is deliberately non-episodic --- Burda et al.\ make it so, and CleanRL follows them
by hardcoding the intrinsic terminal mask to $1.0$ --- and that is exactly what lets the fabricated
row leak. Perturbing only the fabricated row's intrinsic reward and re-running the file's own
generalized-advantage code moves the intrinsic advantage of \emph{every preceding row in the
rollout}, decaying as $(\gamma_{\mathrm{int}}\lambda)^{\Delta} \approx 0.94^{\Delta}$: full amplitude
at the row itself, about half ten rows earlier, still measurable at the head of a $128$-step rollout.
So a defect in $1.2\%$ of rows perturbs a large fraction of the intrinsic advantages, which is the
signal the whole method rests on. Whether that changes the final score is a separate question this
run does not answer; the claim here is only that ``it does not seem to impact performance'' was never
tested and is not obviously true.

\paragraph{The correction.}
Two edits, both keyed on $\texttt{dones}[t]$, which is the only reliable detector. The obvious
alternative --- reading envpool's \texttt{elapsed\_step} for a zero --- fails under
\texttt{episodic\_life=True}, because a lost life consumes a step without resetting the counter; on a
$600$-step probe it found $4$ of the $24$ consumed rows.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Carry the advantage through the consumed row} so that row $t-1$ chains to row $t+1$ as
  though the row were absent. This stops the backward leak in the intrinsic stream.
\item \textbf{Drop the consumed rows from the update batch}, so no loss is ever evaluated on a
  transition that did not happen. The batch is then walked as exactly \texttt{num\_minibatches} equal
  slices with the remainder dropped; a ragged final slice of one row makes the advantage
  normalization divide by a zero standard deviation, and the resulting NaN reaches the weights within
  one optimizer step. The surviving-row count is then held at a fixed allowance rather than varying
  with the number of rows dropped, so every update trains on an equal-sized batch.
\end{enumerate}

Neither upgrading envpool nor switching to its Gymnasium-style interface avoids this. The behaviour
is unchanged in every release through $1.2.5$, and Gymnasium $1.0$ moved its own vector API
\emph{to} next-step auto-reset to match envpool --- so the ``upstream fix'' the documentation waits
for is not coming, because envpool's behaviour became the standard. The run also corrects a smaller
consequence of the same defect: the episode-length counter in CleanRL's statistics wrapper
incremented on consumed steps, overstating episode length by one per life lost.

\section{Distance to ground truth metrics}
\label{sec:metrics}

Each metric compares a method's bonus field $p$ to the oracle field $g$ over the grid
$G$ and returns a single scalar distance $D$ (logged as \texttt{distance\_to\_gt/*}).
Distance is only logged for methods whose bonus field can be built without actions
(\texttt{no\_exploration}, the two count methods, \texttt{rnd\_next\_state},
\texttt{rnd\_next\_state\_position\_only}, \texttt{rnd\_state},
\texttt{rnd\_linear\_next\_state}); action-conditioned methods are skipped.
Table~\ref{tab:metrics} summarizes the six metrics; the three families are detailed below.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.25}
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.4cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.7cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.0cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{4.0cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Family} & \textbf{Metric} & \textbf{Formula} & \textbf{What it measures} \\
\midrule
\multirow{2}{2.4cm}{Scale-fit} & \texttt{min\_c\_l1\_diff} & $\min_c\lVert g-c\,p\rVert_1$ & Best-scaled $L_1$ mismatch to the GT field. \\
 & \texttt{min\_c\_l2\_diff} & $\min_c\lVert g-c\,p\rVert_2$ & Best-scaled $L_2$ mismatch (closed-form $c$). \\
\midrule
\multirow{2}{2.4cm}{Inverse-ratio} & \texttt{min\_c\_l1\_inv} & $\min_c\lVert c\mathbf{1}-z\rVert_1$ & $L_1$ spread of the ratio $z=p\oslash g$ ($c^\star=\mathrm{median}$). \\
 & \texttt{min\_c\_l2\_inv} & $\min_c\lVert c\mathbf{1}-z\rVert_2$ & $L_2$ spread of the ratio $z$ ($c^\star=\mathrm{mean}$). \\
\midrule
\multirow{2}{2.4cm}{Normalized geometry} & \texttt{normalized\_l2} & $\lVert\hat{g}-\hat{p}\rVert_2$ & $L_2$ distance after max-absolute scaling. \\
 & \texttt{normalized\_\allowbreak angle\_\allowbreak rad} & $\arccos\langle u_g,u_p\rangle$ & Angle between unit vectors, in $[0,\pi]$. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Distance-to-ground-truth metrics: each compares a method's bonus field $p$ to the
oracle field $g$ over the grid $G$ and returns a scalar distance $D$. The symbols $z$,
$\hat{g}$, $\hat{p}$, $u_g$, $u_p$ are defined in the respective subsections below.}
\label{tab:metrics}
\end{table}
\clearpage

\subsection{Scale-fit metrics}
\label{sec:dist-scale}

The scale-fit family is invariant to the overall magnitude of $p$: it fits a single
scalar $c$ and reports the residual, $D = \min_{c}\lVert g - c\,p\rVert$.

\paragraph{\texttt{min\_c\_l1\_diff}.}
\begin{align}
D = \min_{c}\lVert g - c\,p\rVert_1 = \min_c\sum_i|g_i - c\,p_i|,
\end{align}
with $c$ found by bounded 1-D numerical search. An exact minimizer is in fact available: $c^\star$ is the weighted median of the ratios $g_i/p_i$ with weights $|p_i|$ (the $L_1$ counterpart of the $L_2$ projection above), so the numerical search is a convenience, not a necessity. If $p\equiv0$, $D = \lVert g\rVert_1$.

\paragraph{\texttt{min\_c\_l2\_diff}.}
Closed-form least-squares scale:
\begin{align}
D = \lVert g - c^\star p\rVert_2, \qquad c^\star = \frac{\langle g,p\rangle}{\langle p,p\rangle}.
\end{align}
\emph{Why this $c^\star$.} It is the minimizer of $\min_c\lVert g-c\,p\rVert_2$: the squared objective $f(c)=\lVert g-c\,p\rVert_2^2=\langle g,g\rangle-2c\,\langle g,p\rangle+c^2\langle p,p\rangle$ is a convex quadratic in the scalar $c$, so its stationary point $f'(c)=-2\langle g,p\rangle+2c\,\langle p,p\rangle=0$---that is, $c^\star=\langle g,p\rangle/\langle p,p\rangle$---is the global minimum (the quadratic opens upward because $\langle p,p\rangle\ge0$). If $p\equiv0$, $D = \lVert g\rVert_2$.
\clearpage

\subsection{Inverse-ratio metrics}
\label{sec:dist-inv}

These ask whether $p$ is a single constant multiple of $g$ \emph{cell by cell}. Form the
element-wise ratio $z = p \oslash g$ (with $g$ regularized so $|g_i|\ge\varepsilon=10^{-8}$,
sign preserved), fit one constant $c$, and report $D = \min_{c}\lVert c\mathbf{1} - z\rVert$.
A small value means $p/g$ is nearly constant across all cells.

\paragraph{\texttt{min\_c\_l1\_inv}.}
The $L_1$ minimizer is the median:
\begin{align}
D = \sum_i|c^\star - z_i|, \qquad c^\star = \mathrm{median}(z).
\end{align}

\paragraph{\texttt{min\_c\_l2\_inv}.}
The $L_2$ minimizer is the mean:
\begin{align}
D = \lVert c^\star\mathbf{1} - z\rVert_2, \qquad c^\star = \bar{z}.
\end{align}
Equivalent to $\sqrt{n}$ times the standard deviation of $z$.
\clearpage

\subsection{Normalized-geometry metrics}
\label{sec:dist-norm}

Rescale both vectors to a common scale, then compare their shape. One uses max-absolute
scaling and an $L_2$ difference; the other compares directions via the angle between unit
vectors.

\paragraph{\texttt{normalized\_l2}.}
\begin{align}
D = \Big\lVert \frac{g}{\max_i|g_i|+\varepsilon} - \frac{p}{\max_i|p_i|+\varepsilon}\Big\rVert_2,
\end{align}
with $\varepsilon=10^{-10}$ (each vector's peak magnitude scaled to $\approx1$).

\paragraph{\texttt{normalized\_angle\_rad}.}
\begin{align}
D = \arccos\!\big(\mathrm{clip}(\langle u_g, u_p\rangle, -1, 1)\big)\in[0,\pi],
\end{align}
where $u_g = g/(\lVert g\rVert_2+\varepsilon)$ and $u_p = p/(\lVert p\rVert_2+\varepsilon)$
are the unit directions ($\varepsilon=10^{-10}$). $0$ = same direction, $\pi$ = opposite.
\clearpage


\subsection{Metric applicability by algorithm}
\label{sec:metric-applicability}

Not every algorithm yields a bonus field that can be compared to the oracle, so not every
metric can be computed for every algorithm. Table~\ref{tab:metric-applicability} marks, for
each of the eleven \texttt{--algorithm} choices, which of the six distance metrics are computed
(\ding{51}; a blank cell means the metric is not computed for that algorithm). The split is
decided per algorithm rather than per metric: the dispatcher
(\texttt{compute\_intrinsic\_vector\_distance}) computes \emph{all six} metrics for an
algorithm whose bonus is a function of the state (or next state) alone, and \emph{none} for an
action-conditioned algorithm (it returns nothing, so \texttt{distance\_to\_gt/*} is never
logged). The reason for each algorithm is enumerated below.

  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{1.25cm}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{0.9cm}

}
\caption{Which distance-to-ground-truth metrics are computed for each algorithm.
\ding{51}~= computed (logged under \texttt{distance\_to\_gt/*}); a blank cell = not computed.
The seven columns left of the rule are the algorithms whose bonus field is evaluable without
actions (\texttt{ALGORITHMS\_NO\_ACTION}), for which all six metrics are computed; the four
columns to the right are action-conditioned and are skipped entirely. \texttt{gt\_position\_velocity}
is the oracle field $g$ itself, so its six values are self-distances (numerically $\approx 0$).}
\label{tab:metric-applicability}
\end{table}

Each algorithm and the reason all six / none of the metrics apply to it:

\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=3pt]
\item \texttt{no\_exploration} --- \emph{all six.} The bonus is identically zero, a constant
field over the grid, so $p$ is the zero vector and every metric is well-defined (the scale-fit
and inverse-ratio metrics reduce to $\lVert g\rVert$ or to $0$).
\item \texttt{gt\_position} --- \emph{all six.} The oracle visit-count bonus keyed on position
is a fixed function of the cell and needs no action, so $p$ is defined on every grid cell.
\item \texttt{gt\_position\_velocity} --- \emph{all six.} This is the ground-truth field $g$
itself; every metric is defined and returns a self-distance ($\approx 0$).
\item \texttt{rnd\_next\_state} --- \emph{all six.} The bonus is the predictor--target error on
the next state; each grid cell serves as its own next state, so the field is evaluable without
actions.
\item \texttt{rnd\_next\_state\_position\_only} --- \emph{all six.} As \texttt{rnd\_next\_state},
but on the position slice of the next state; still a state-only field.
\item \texttt{rnd\_state} --- \emph{all six.} The bonus is the predictor--target error on the
current state, a function of the cell alone.
\item \texttt{rnd\_linear\_next\_state} --- \emph{all six.} Linear-head RND on the next state;
the readout still depends only on the (next) state, so the field is defined per cell.
\item \texttt{rnd\_state\_action} --- \emph{none.} The bonus is a function of $(s,a)$; without
fixing an action there is no single value per grid cell, so it cannot be compared to the
state-indexed oracle field $g$.
\item \texttt{rnd\_state\_action\_next\_state} --- \emph{none.} The input concatenates the
state, the action, and the next state reached by that action, so the bonus is not a function of
the grid cell alone.
\item \texttt{rnd\_elliptical} --- \emph{none.} The elliptical (Mahalanobis) bonus
$\sqrt{\phi(s,a)^\top\Lambda^{-1}\phi(s,a)}$ is computed on state--action features and
accumulates its covariance $\Lambda$ from actions, so it has no action-free per-cell value to
compare against $g$.
\item \texttt{rnd\_\allowbreak elliptical\_\allowbreak global} --- \emph{none.} The same
state--action bonus as \texttt{rnd\_elliptical}, differing only in that the covariance $\Lambda$ is
the global (cumulative) running matrix rather than the per-batch one; it is still action-conditioned,
so it likewise has no action-free per-cell value to compare against $g$.
\end{enumerate}
